E E -theory of X X - C ∗ C^{*} -algebras and functor formalisms
Ulrich Bunke

Abstract
We show that E E -theory for locally compact Hausdorff spaces constitutes a six-functor formalism which is equivalent to the six-functor formalism of E \mathrm{E} -valued sheaves. We furthermore show that the E E -theory category for locales that can be written as unions of finite open sublocales is equivalent to the category of E \mathrm{E} -valued cosheaves.



1
Introduction

To every locale
X
one can functorially associate the
E
-theory category
E(X)
. It is a presentable stable
\infty
-category defined as the target of the universal
homological functor on the category of
X
-
C^{*}
-algebras –
C^{*}
-algebras equipped with a morphism of locales

I(A)\to X
, where
I(A)
is the locale of closed
*
-ideals of
A
. Our first main result describes the category
E(X)

for ”sufficiently finite” locales in homotopy-theoretic terms:

Theorem 1.1

(
Theorem

1.4
)
.

For a locale
X
that can be written as a union of finite open sublocales, there is a natural equivalence

E(X)\simeq\mathrm{CoShv}(X,\mathrm{E})\ .

Turning to the topological setting, our second result extends this homotopy-theoretic perspective to locally compact Hausdorff spaces:

Theorem 1.2

(
Theorem

1.10
)
.

The assignment
X\mapsto E(X)
is part of a six-functor formalism
on
\mathrm{LCH}
that is equivalent to the
\mathrm{E}
-valued sheaf-theoretic six-functor formalism

X\mapsto\mathrm{Shv}(X,\mathrm{E})
, where
\mathrm{E}:=E(*)
.

We will give more precise statements below after
providing a historical introduction to the field.

Non-commutative homotopy theory in the sense of the present paper is the study of
C^{*}
-algebras up to homotopy invariance and
K
-stability.
A fundamental invariant in this field is the topological
K
-theory of
C^{*}
-algebras
which plays a central role in the classification of
C^{*}
-algebras, index theory, algebraic topology, mathematical physics and other fields. By axiomatizing core properties of
K
-theory through the notion of a homological functor, one is led to study universal homological functors, specifically
KK
-theory or
E
-theory.
These concepts extend beyond plain
C^{*}
-algebras to categories of
C^{*}
-algebras with additional structures like actions by groups, groupoids, tensor categories, as well as parametrization over a space.
The primary objective of the present paper is to describe the
E
-theory category
E(X)
of the category
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of
X
-
C^{*}
algebras – that is,
C^{*}
-algebras parametrized by a topological space or, more generally, a locale
X
.

KK
-theory was introduced in
[
Kas88
]
as a bivariant functor
(A,B)\mapsto KK(A,B)
on
C^{*}
-algebras sending

A,B
to the group of equivalence classes of Kasparov modules (see
[
Bla98
]
for a comprehensive account). Through the Kasparov product,
this bivariant functor can be interpreted as a functor from
C^{*}
-algebras to an additive category.
The
E
-theory functor was first constructed in
[
Hig90a
]
as the universal homotopy invariant,
K
-stable
and exact functor from the category of
C^{*}
-algebras to an additive category.
The fundamental distinction between
KK
-and
E
-theory lies in their respective exactness properties: while
KK
-theory is exact for
exact sequences of
C^{*}
-algebras admitting a completely positive contractive split, the

E
-theory functor is exact for all exact sequences.
For separable
C^{*}
-algebras,
E
-theory was subsequently reformulated
in
[
CH90
]
using homotopy classes of asymptotic morphisms.
This framework was later extended to non-separable and equivariant settings in
[
GHT00
]
, although in those cases exactness remains tied to separability assumptions.

For a locally compact Hausdorff space
X
, the additive
E
-theory category
E(X)
for
C_{0}(X)
-algebras was introduced in
[
PT00
]
for the separable case, and subsequently extended in
[
Pop04
]
, also
to include the groupoid-equivariant setting.
These works further developed the maximal balanced tensor product
\otimes_{X}
of
C_{0}(X)
-algebras, the induced tensor bifunctor on the
E
-theory category, and the contravariant functoriality for maps between locally compact Hausdorff spaces. Note that
KK
-theory for
C_{0}(X)
-algebras was already introduced in the
in the seminal paper
[
Kas88
]
.

An alternative characterization of
C_{0}(X)
-algebras, which does not explicitly involve the algebra
C_{0}(X)
, was provided in

[
DM12
]
. This new definition can be interpreted for arbitrary topological spaces –
or even locales – giving rise to the notion of
X
-
C^{*}
-algebras. The paper
[
DM12
]
extends the construction of the additive
E
-theory category

E(X)
for separable
X
-
C^{*}
-algebras from
second countable locally compact Hausdorff spaces to general second countable
topological spaces, using the framework of asymptotic morphisms.
This generalization facilitates the study of the
E
-theory category
E(X)
for finite topological spaces,
which is more amenable to explicit homotopy-theoretic calculations.

It was observed in
[
MN06
]
that universal homological
functors on
C^{*}
-algebras naturally take values in triangulated categories, a fact that also applies to the
E
-theory category
E(X)
.
In
[
DM12
]
the dependence of
E(X)
on the space
X
was further investigated, revealing
adjunctions for open and closed embeddings. This work also initiated the explicit calculation of the mapping groups
of
E(X)
. A key result is

[
DM12
, Thm. 3.2]
which expresses the mapping groups of
E(X)
in terms of the mapping groups in
E(Y_{i})
for a family
of finite quotients
(X\to Y_{i})_{i}
. Consequently
the collection of evaluation functors

({\mathtt{ev}}_{U}:E(X)\to{\mathbf{Ab}})_{U\in\mathrm{Open}(X)}

is shown to be jointly conservative
in
[
DM12
, Thm. 3.10]
. This result further motivates the problem of describing the mapping groups
of
E(Y)
for finite topological spaces
Y
. While initial results were obtained in
[
DV14
]
,
[
Ben14
]
(see also
[
MN08
]
where the same principles were applied in the case of
KK
-theory),
complete calculations remain restricted to spaces with very few points – typically fewer than three –
or to posets
\mathrm{Open}(Y)
with a very simple structure, such as
[n]
. From the perspective of the present paper the primary reason for these limitations is that working solely with triangulated categories neglects crucial
higher structural information.

Most known triangulated categories arise as the homotopy categories of stable
\infty
-categories, a
principle that also applies to
KK
- and
E
-theory.
Building on developments for
KK
-theory in
[
LN18
]
,
[
BEL
]
in
[
Bun24
]

the
E
-theory for
C^{*}
-algebras was redefined as the universal
functor to a cocomplete stable
\infty
-category which is homotopy invariant,

K
-stable, exact and s-finitary. This universal functor can be constructed by iteratively forcing these universal properties.

An alternative characterization and construction of

E
-theory as a universal homotopy invariant,
K
-stable, exact and filtered colimit-preserving functor

\mathrm{e}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{E}

(1.1)

from
C^{*}
-algebras to a cocomplete stable
\infty
-category
was given in
[
BD24
]
(including the equivariant case for discrete groups).
In
[
Bun24
]
, it was further verified – by comparing the universal property of its
homotopy category as an additive category with the universal property of the
E
-theory from
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.6]
– that the composition

\mathrm{ho}\circ\mathrm{e}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{E}\to\mathrm{ho}(\mathrm{E})

is equivalent to the classical
E
-theory functor.

The reason that the aforementioned references at certain points restrict to separable
C^{*}
-algebras, and in consequence to second countable topological spaces,
lies in the details of the construction of the composition of asymptotic morphisms following
[
CH90
]
,
and the Connes-Higson construction of asymptotic morphisms from exact sequences.

A functor which is homotopy invariant,
K
-stable, and preserves both exact sequences and filtered colimits will be called homological (see
2.7
). In order to axiomatize this, we introduce in
2.3
the concept of an
E
-theory context as a category for which we can formulate the conditions of a homological functor. We further define morphisms
between
E
-theory contexts such that precompositon with such a morphism preserves homological functors.
This gives rise to the category
\mathcal{EC}
of
E
-theory contexts.

In
Theorem

2.10
, following the general path for the construction of universal homological functors
as in
[
MV99
]
,
[
Øst10
]
,
[
BGT13
]
, we show that there exists a functor

\mathrm{E}:\mathcal{EC}\to\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}^{L}

(1.2)

from the category of
E
-theory contexts
\mathcal{EC}
to the category
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
of presentable stable
\infty
-categories
and left-adjoint functors. This functor is equipped
with a natural transformation
\mathrm{e}:{\mathtt{id}}\to\mathrm{E}
(where
\mathrm{E}
is regarded as
{\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty}
-valued),
such that, for every
E
-theory context
{\mathcal{A}}
in
\mathcal{EC}
,
the functor

\mathrm{e}_{{\mathcal{A}}}:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}})

is the universal homological functor for
{\mathcal{A}}
.
In
Theorem

2.18
we further extend this construction to incorporate lax symmetric
monoidal structures.

Note that our construction of the
E
-theory functor is designed to yield the universal homological functor. It does not involve any explicit description of the mapping spaces in terms of asymptotic morphisms or similar constructions.
However, as explained in
[
Bun24
, Sec. 14]
one can use asymptotic morphisms to represent points in the mapping spaces. In cases where a classical construction exists – for example, if
{\mathcal{A}}=XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
for a topological space
X
–
a comparison of the mapping groups of
\mathrm{ho}(\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}}))
with the mapping groups of the classical triangulated category
could in principle be achieved by comparing the universal properties of the categories. While we expect that the
E
-theory groups constructed in the present paper coincide with the classical
E
-theory groups in the cases where both are defined, we will not provide a formal proof. One reason is the lack of formulated sufficiently general universal properties of the classical constructions – analogous to
[
Hig90a
, Thm. 3.6]
–
which could be compared with the ones of our construction.

In this paper we consider
E
-theory contexts
{\mathcal{A}}
consisting of
C^{*}
-algebras with additional structure, and where morphisms
are structure-preserving homomorphisms between
C^{*}
-algebras. We focus particularly on
systems of ideals parametrized by a poset
P
admiting a maximal element, leading to the
E
-theory context of
P
-
C^{*}
-algebras
PC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Within this framework, we may impose further conditions on the
parametrization, most notably :

1.

left exact: non-empty finite meets are sent to intersections of ideals

2.

regular: non-empty finite meets are sent to intersections and filtered joins are sent to closures of unions of ideals

3.

continuous: finite meets are sent to intersections and joins are sent to closures of sums of ideals

These conditions lead to a sequence of sub-
E
-theory contexts

P^{\mathrm{c}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\subseteq P^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\subseteq P^{\mathrm{le}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\subseteq PC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ .

(1.3)

For a topological space
X
the
E
-theory context

\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{c}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
recovers the definition of the category of
X
-
C^{*}
-algebras given in
[
DM12
]
.

Section

3
is devoted to the study of categorical properties of these subcategories.
Their understanding is crucial because a homological functor on an
E
-theory context
{\mathcal{A}}
must preserve those filtered colimits and must send those exact sequences to fibre sequences which exist in
{\mathcal{A}}
. Filtered colimits and exact sequences (i.e., bifibre sequences) are characterized in terms of the categorical language of
{\mathcal{A}}
. To verify that a given functor is homological we must therefore identify these structures explicitly.
As indicated in (
1.3
), the
E
-theory context
{\mathcal{A}}
usually arises as a subcategory of an ambient category of
C^{*}
-algebras with structures in which
limits and colimits are well-understood.
In this situation we must know that there are no ”unexpected” filtered colimits or exact sequences
in
{\mathcal{A}}
– that is, structures that do not coincides with those in the ambient category.

In
homotopy theory, the presentability of a category is a very convenient property, as it implies the existence of all limits and colimits and allows for straightforward applications of adjoint functor theorems.
Using standard arguments, we observe that the
presentablility of
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
implies that of the
categories
P^{\mathrm{le}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
PC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, when viewed as subcategories of the functor category
{\mathbf{Fun}}(P,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
. The regular and the continuous cases are more involved. The following result, which establishes their presentability under additional assumptions, may be of independent interest.

Theorem 1.3
.

1.

(
Theorem

3.17
) If
P
is a stably locally compact frame, then
P^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is presentable.

2.

(
Theorem

5.15
) If
P=\mathrm{Open}(X)
for a locally compact Hausdorff space
X
, then
P^{c}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is presentable.

There is an adjunction
F:\mathbf{Poset}\leftrightarrows\mathbf{Frame}:\mathrm{incl}
,
where
F(Q)
is the free frame on the poset
Q
. The functor
F
preserves the property of having a maximal element.
We then have
QC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\simeq F(Q)^{\mathrm{c}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
(see
[
MN09
, Sec. 2.9]
). Therefore
P^{\mathrm{c}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is presentable if
P\cong F(Q)
for some
poset
Q
. It remains an interesting question whether, in these statements, the assumptions on
P
can be further relaxed, beyond these obvious cases. Our current proof of
Theorem

5.15
heavily relies on the specific structure of a locally compact Hausdorff space and the explicit use of the algebra
C_{0}(X)
.

Following
[
DM12
]
, we associate
to any topological space
X
the
E
-theory context

XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:=\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{c}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ .

This definition naturally extends to the setting of locales, yielding a functor

(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathbf{Locale}\to\mathcal{EC}\ ,\quad X\mapsto XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,\quad f\mapsto f_{!}

(1.4)

from locales to
E
-theory contexts (see
3.26
).
By composing this assignment with the
E
-theory functor defined in (
1.2
) we obtain
the functor

E:\mathbf{Locale}\to\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}\ ,\quad X\mapsto E(X)\ ,\quad f\mapsto f_{!}\ ,

see
3.27
.

We return to the problem of describing the category
E(X)
explicitly.
Our first main theorem provides a complete homotopy-theoretic characterization of
E(X)
for ”sufficiently small” locales
X
. The definition of cosheaves
\mathrm{CoShv}(X,{\mathcal{D}})
with values in a presentable stable
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{D}}
on a locale
X
is recalled in
4.14
.
For every
A
in
E(X)
and open
U
in
X
we have an evaluation

A(U)
in
\mathrm{E}
. The assignment
U\mapsto A(U)
turns out to be an
\mathrm{E}
-valued cosheaf
on
X
which we will denoted by
s_{X}(A)
.

Theorem 1.4

(
Theorem

4.18
.
2
and
4.22
)
.

If
X
is a locale that can be written as a filtered union of finite open sublocales, then there is an equivalence

s_{X}:E(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\mathrm{CoShv}(X,\mathrm{E})\ .

For interesting infinite topological spaces, even those as simple as the unit interval
[0,1]
,
finding an explicit description of the category

E(X)
has remained an open problem, although some calculations of the
morphism groups
\pi_{0}{\mathtt{map}}_{E([0,1])}(A,B)
for elementary
C([0,1])
-algebras

A
and
B
are known
[
DV14
]
.

It turns out that considering the entire functor
X\mapsto E(X)
at once simplifies the problem considerably, at least for locally compact Hausdorff spaces. By specialization to locally compact Hausdorff spaces the functor
(
1.4
) gives rise to a functor

(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{LCH}\to\mathcal{EC}\ ,\quad X\mapsto XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,\quad f\mapsto f_{!}\ .

(1.5)

In
5.40
we equip the assignment

\mathrm{LCH}\ni X\mapsto XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\in\mathcal{EC}

with a contravariant functoriality and an external tensor product derived from the maximal tensor product of
C^{*}
-algebras. Parts of these structures have already been considered in
[
EW98
]
,

[
PT00
]
and
[
Pop04
]
.

Proposition 1.5

(
5.40
)
.

There is a lax symmetric monoidal functor to
E
-theory contexts (in the sense of
2.14
)

(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}\ ,\quad X\mapsto XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,\quad f\mapsto f^{*}\ .

To formally describe the interaction of the covariant functoriality with the contravariant functoriality and the tensor products
we employ the language of functor formalisms. Following
[
GR17
]
,
[
DG22
]
,
[
HM24
]
,
[
DK25
]
,
[
Sch25
]
,
[
Zhu25
]
we
adopt the concept of a functor formalism on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
of locally compact Hausdorff spaces, open inclusions, and proper maps. We refer to
6.7
for the notion of a three-functor formalism on
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
.
The three functors are
f^{*}
and
f_{!}
for all morphisms in
\mathrm{LCH}
and
the tensor product
\otimes_{X}
for all
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
. To constitute a three-functor formalism, these must satisfy various compatibility conditions detailed in
6.7
.

Theorem 1.6

(
Theorem

5.2
)
.

The lax symmetric monoidal functor
from
1.5
is a three-functor formalism on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
.

By composing the lax symmetric monoidal extension of the
E
-theory functor from (
1.2
) with the functor from
1.5
we get a lax symmetric monoidal functor

E:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}\ ,\quad X\mapsto E(X)\ ,\quad f\mapsto f^{*}\ .

(1.6)

Exploiting the fact that
E
takes values in presentable
\infty
-categories
and left adjoint functors we arrive at:

Theorem 1.7

(
Theorem

6.12
)
.

The lax symmetric monoidal functor
E
in
(
1.6
) is a presentable six-functor formalism on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
.

The six functors are the functors
f^{*}
,
f_{!}
and
\otimes_{X}
from above, their right-adjoints
f_{*}
,
f^{!}
, and the interal hom-functor
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{X}
. We refer to
6.9
for the complete definition of a six-functor formalism.
Furthermore, the result of
[
HM24
, Prop. 3.3.3]
or
[
DK25
, Thm 3.3]
ensures that our definition of a six-functor formalism is equivalent to the definition of a six-functor formalism as a lax symmetric monoidal functor defined on a span category associated to the geometric context
(\mathrm{LCH},\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll})

[
GR17
]
,
[
DK25
, Def. 2.4]
,
[
Sch25
, Def. 2.5]
,
[
HM24
, Def. 1.2.4]
, where the class of
!
-able maps, denoted by
\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll}
, consists of all maps.

The classical six-functor formalism on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
, that agrees with
\mathrm{E}
on the one-point space
\mathrm{pt}
, is the sheaf-theoretic six-functor formalism

\mathrm{Shv}(-,\mathrm{E}):\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}},\quad X\mapsto\mathrm{Shv}(X,\mathrm{E})\ ,\quad f\mapsto\hat{f}^{*}

(see
[
Vol25
]
for a complete construction). In
[
BV
]
we characterize
\mathrm{Shv}(-,{\mathcal{D}})
for
{\mathcal{D}}
in
{\mathbf{C}^{\ast}\mathbf{Alg}}(\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}})
with dualizable underlying object among six-functor formalisms
D
with
D(\mathrm{pt})={\mathcal{D}}

by universal properties. This characterization is recalled here as
Theorem

6.28
and rests on three conditions.

The first condition is that the six-functor formalism
D
is a coefficient system
[
Zhu25
]
(see also
[
DG22
]
for the origin of this concept in algebraic geometry). Being a coefficient system is equivalent to the canonical descent condition described in
6.16
.

Theorem 1.8

(
Theorem

6.17
)
.

The six-functor formalism
E
in (
1.6
) is a coefficient system.

The second condition for
Theorem

6.28
concerns the associated
cohomological functor
\Gamma^{D}
(see
6.25
). In the case of the six-functor formalism of sheaves
\mathrm{Shv}(-,{\mathcal{D}})
it sends
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
to the usual sheaf cohomology

\Gamma^{\mathrm{Shv}(-,{\mathcal{D}})}(X,1)\in{\mathcal{D}}

of the constant sheaf on
X

with value
1
, the tensor unit of
{\mathcal{D}}
. In the case of the six-functor formalism
E
from (
1.6
) the associated cohomological functor sends

X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
to

\Gamma^{E}(X)=\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{K\subseteq X}\mathrm{e}(C(K))\in\mathrm{E}\ ,

where the limit runs over all compact subsets of
X
. The second condition requires the associated cohomological functor
to be finitary in the sense of
6.26
, i.e., it must send cofiltered systems of compact Hausdorff spaces to colimits. While the detailed argument for the continuity of
\Gamma^{\mathrm{Shv}(-,{\mathcal{D}})}
is quite involved
(see
[
KNP
, Thm. 3.10]
), the argument for the continuity of
\Gamma^{E}
given in the proof of
6.27
is considerably simpler and merely uses Gelfand duality and the fact (true by definition) that the
E
-theory functor
(
1.1
) preserves filtered colimits.

The third condition for
Theorem

6.28
is that the six-functor formalism
D
is section-determined (see
6.21
). For
E
the classical analogue of this property was verified in
[
DM12
, Thm. 3.10]
.

Theorem 1.9

(
Theorem

6.24
)
.

The functor
E
in (
6.3
) is section-determined.

Our proof of
Theorem

1.9
relies on the same idea as that of

[
DM12
, Thm. 3.10]
, but technically it is completely independent.

We can now apply
Theorem

6.28
in order to obtain the second main theorem of the present paper:

Theorem 1.10

(
6.29
)
.

There is a natural equivalence of six-functor formalisms

{\mathcal{B}}:\mathrm{Shv}(-,\mathrm{E})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}E(-):\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}\ .

(1.7)

This equivalence provides complete homotopy-theoretic characterization of
E(X)
and its associated functorial operations. Furthermore it enables
the transfer of established results and constructions from sheaf theory to
E
-theory of
X
-
C^{*}
-algebras – a direction which will be explored
in subsequent papers.

As an immediate consequence, we highlight the following result regarding dualizability.
Since
\mathrm{E}
is known to be a dualizable presentable stable
\infty
-category by
[
BD24
]
, and it has been established that this property extends to
\mathrm{Shv}(X,\mathrm{E})
for any locally compact Hausdorff space
X

[
Efi24
]
(see also
[
KNP
]
for a detailed argument) we obtain:

Corollary 1.11
.

For every locally compact Hausdorff space the presentable stable
\infty
-category

E(X)
is dualizable.

We note that the method employed in
[
BD24
]
to prove the dualizability of
\mathrm{E}
does not admit a direct generalization to the case of
E(X)
.

If
F
is in
\mathrm{Shv}(X,\mathrm{E})
, then we have the equivalence
{\mathcal{B}}(F)(x)\simeq F_{x}
,
where for
A
in
E(X)
we let
A(x):=s_{X}(A)(X)/s_{X}(A)(X\setminus\{x\})
denote the fibre of
A
at
x
, and

F_{x}
denotes the stalk of the sheaf
F
at
x
. Recall that a locally compact Hausdorff space

X
is called hypercomplete if equivalences in
\mathrm{Shv}(X,\mathbf{Ani})
can be detected on stalks. For example, finite-dimensional spaces are hypercomplete. If
X
is hypercomplete, then equivalences in any sheaf category taking values in a dualizable target can be detected on stalks. In particular, this applies to
\mathrm{Shv}(X,\mathrm{E})
.
The following consequence of
Theorem

1.10
is an analog of
[
Dad09
, Thm. 1.1]
:

Corollary 1.12
.

If the locally compact Hausdorff space
X
is hypercomplete, then
the collection of fibres
(E(X)\ni A\mapsto A(x)\in\mathrm{E})_{x\in X}
is jointly conservative.

Note that
Theorem

1.4
and
Theorem

1.10
describe
E(X)
modulo the knowledge of
the symmetric monoidal category
\mathrm{E}
in
{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}^{L})
itself. The latter contains a full subcategory, called the bootstrap class, which is equivalent to the category
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{KU}(\mathbf{Sp})
known from homotopy theory. The
bootstrap class
\mathrm{B}\subseteq\mathrm{E}
is the image of the symmetric monoidal left-adjoint
in the adjunction

B:{\mathbf{Mod}}_{KU}(\mathbf{Sp})\leftrightarrows\mathrm{E}:K:={\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{E}}(1,-)\ ,

(1.8)

whose right adjoint is the lax symmetric monoidal
K
-theory functor.
Crucially, the calculation

{\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{E}}(1,1)\simeq KU

implies that
\mathrm{E}
is enriched in
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{KU}(\mathbf{Sp})
. This, in turn, implies that
the six-functor formalism
E
(
1.6
) actually takes values in
KU
-module enriched stable
\infty
-categories. If
A
is in the bootstrap class of
\mathrm{E}
, then
the UCT and the Künneth formula

{\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{E}}(A,B)\simeq{\mathtt{map}}_{KU}(K(A),K(B))\ ,\quad K(A\otimes B)\simeq K(A)\otimes_{KU}K(B)

for all
B
in
\mathrm{E}

are formal consequences of the definitions.
More generally we say that
A
in
\mathrm{E}
belongs to the Künneth class if the canonical map

K(A)\otimes_{KU}K(B)\to K(A\otimes B)

is an equivalence for every
B
in
\mathrm{E}
.
The problem of defining a bootstrap class in
E(X)
and to obtain a UCT for finite spaces
X
has already been considered in

[
MN09
]
.
In view of
4.20
, for locales
X
that are unions of finite open sublocales we could define the bootstrap class
B(X)\subseteq E(X)

as the preimage of the full subcategory

\mathrm{CoShv}(X,{\mathbf{Mod}}_{KU}(\mathbf{Sp}))\subseteq\mathrm{CoShv}(X,\mathrm{E})

under the equivalence
s_{X}
, where the inclusion above is realized by the pointwise application of the colimit-preserving functor
B
from (
1.8
).

Definition 1.13
.

We define the
K
-theory cosheaf functor

c\mathcal{K}:(K\circ-)\circ s_{X}:E(X)\to\mathrm{CoShv}(X,{\mathbf{Mod}}_{KU}(\mathbf{Sp}))\ .

The following is the general version of the universal coefficient theorem for
E(X)
.

Corollary 1.14
.

Assume that
X
is a locale that is the union of finite open sublocales.
If
A
is in the bootstrap class
B(X)
, then we have

{\mathtt{map}}_{E(X)}(A,B)\simeq{\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{CoShv}(X,{\mathbf{Mod}}_{KU}(\mathbf{Sp}))}(c\mathcal{K}(A),c\mathcal{K}(B))

for all
B
in
E(X)
.

Remark 1.15
.

For a locally compact Hausdorff space
X

one can show that the composition

{\mathcal{V}}\circ\mathcal{K}_{X}:E(X)\to\mathrm{Shv}(X,\mathrm{E})\to\mathrm{CoShv}(X,\mathrm{E})

of the Verdier duality functor and the right-adjoint (or inverse) of
{\mathcal{B}}_{X}:\mathrm{Shv}(X,\mathrm{E})\to E(X)
from

Theorem

1.10
is equivalent to the functor
s_{X}
from
Theorem

4.18
.
It follows from Verdier duality that

s_{X}:E(X)\to\mathrm{CoShv}(X,\mathrm{E})

is an equivalence also for locally compact Hausdorff spaces.
Consequently, the above considerations and
1.14
remains valid for locally compact Hausdorff spaces
X
. We will discuss this and applications to the Künneth class in future work.

Acknowledgements: The author would like to thank Benjamin Dünzinger, Marco Volpe, and Christoph Winges for many fruitful discussions regarding various aspects of this work.

2
E
-theory contexts and
E
-theory

2.1
E
-theory contexts

In this section, we introduce the concept of an
E
-theory context.
Consider the symmetric monoidal category

\mathcal{N}:=C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep},\mathrm{nucl}}

of separable nuclear
C^{*}
-algebras as an object in
{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Cat}})
and form
the category
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbf{Cat}})_{*}
of pointed
\mathcal{N}
-modules.
Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be in
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbf{Cat}})_{*}
with zero object
0
.

Definition 2.1
.

A commutative square

\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C}

in
{\mathcal{A}}
is called an exact sequence if it is both cartesian and cocartesian.

Definition 2.2
.

A morphism
f:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
in
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbf{Cat}})_{*}
is called
E
-admissible if it preserves finite products, filtered colimits and exact sequences.

We denote the
\mathcal{N}
-module structure of
{\mathcal{A}}
by the tensor product

-\otimes-:{\mathcal{A}}\otimes\mathcal{N}\to{\mathcal{A}}\ .

Definition 2.3
.

1.

An object
{\mathcal{A}}
in
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbf{Cat}})_{*}
is called an
E
-theory context if, for every
N
in
\mathcal{N}
and every
A
in
{\mathcal{A}}
,
the functors

A\otimes-:\mathcal{N}\to{\mathcal{A}}\ ,\qquad-\otimes N:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{A}}

are
E
-admissible.

2.

We let
\mathcal{EC}
denote the subcategory of
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbf{Cat}})_{*}
consisting of
E
-theory contexts and
E
-admissible morphisms.

Note that
\mathcal{EC}
is a 2-category.

Example 2.4
.

The category
\mathcal{N}
itself is an
E
-theory context.
∎

In
Section

3
we will describe a variety of further examples of
E
-theory contexts.

Remark 2.5
.

Filtered colimits, products and exact sequences in
{\mathcal{A}}
are defined purely in terms of the category
{\mathcal{A}}
.
We do not require an
E
-theory context to admit all filtered colimits, finite products, kernels or quotients.
A morphism between
E
-theory contexts is only required to preserve those
filtered colimits, finite products or exact sequences which exist in the domain.

Most of our examples of
E
-theory contexts in
Section

3
arise from diagrams of
C^{*}
-algebras. These examples have natural explicit candidates for filtered colimits, products or exact sequences. When working with these
E
-theory contexts, it is crucial to verify that these ”natural” candidates coincide
with the
categorical constructions.

∎

2.2
The
E
-theory functor

In this section we introduce the notion of a homological functor on an
E
-theory context. We then construct the
E
-theory functor as the universal homological functor.

An
E
-theory context
{\mathcal{A}}
carries a natural topological enrichment such that
for every second countable compact Hausdorff space

W
, there is a natural isomorphism

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathbf{Top}}}(W,{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(A,B))\cong{\mathtt{Hom}}_{{\mathcal{A}}}(A,B\otimes C(W))\ .

The topological enrichment induces a notion of homotopy equivalence in
{\mathcal{A}}
.

Furthermore, we define a left upper corner inclusion for an object
A
in
{\mathcal{A}}
to be a morphism of the form

A\cong A\otimes{\mathbb{C}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{id}}\otimes i}}{{\to}}A\otimes K\ ,

where
i:{\mathbb{C}}\to K
is the morphism in
\mathcal{N}
from
{\mathbb{C}}
into the algebra
K
of compact operators on a separable Hilbert space that sends
1
in
{\mathbb{C}}
to a minimal projection in
K
.

Remark 2.6
.

These definitions rely on the
\mathcal{N}
-module structure of
{\mathcal{A}}
and the fact that both
C(W)
(for a second countable compact Hausdorff space) and
K
belong to
\mathcal{N}
.

∎

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be an
E
-theory context and

F:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
be a functor into a cocomplete stable
\infty
-category.

Definition 2.7
.

The functor
F:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is called homological satisfies the following properties:

1.

F
preserves zero objects.

2.

F
sends homotopy equivalences to equivalences.

3.

F
sends left upper corner inclusions to equivalences.

4.

F
sends exact sequences to fibre sequences.

5.

F
preserves finite products.

6.

F
preserves filtered colimits.

We denote by
{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{homol}}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{C}})
the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{C}})
consisting of homological functors.

Example 2.8
.

The category
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of
C^{*}
-algebras carries the structure of an
E
-theory context, see also
2.19
below. The
K
-theory functor

K:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathbf{Sp}

serves as the primary motivating example of a homological functor.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

In order address size issues,
we fix two consecutive universes, referred to as the universes of small and large sets. We assume that all
C^{*}
-algebras
and the index sets for the filtered colimits in
2.7
in the definitions of
E
-theory contexts and their morphisms belong to the small universe. In contrast,
the functor categories, the categories of spectra
\mathbf{Sp}
and the test categories
{\mathcal{C}}
below belong to the large universe.

∎

Theorem 2.10
.

For every
E
-theory context
{\mathcal{A}}
,
there exists a unique homological functor

\mathrm{e}_{{\mathcal{A}}}:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}})

inducing an equivalence

\mathrm{e}_{{\mathcal{A}}}^{*}:{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}}),{\mathcal{C}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Fun}}^{\mathrm{homol}}({\mathcal{A}},{\mathcal{C}})

(2.1)

for every cocomplete stable
\infty
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Proof.

This proof follows the general procedure for constructing universal homological functors
[
MV99
]
,
[
Øst10
]
,
[
BGT13
]
.
Consider the functor

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Sp})

defined as the composition of the Yoneda embedding with the suspension spectrum functor.
We identify a small set of morphisms in
{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Sp})
consisting of:

1.

0\to\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(0)

2.

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(f)
for all homotopy equivalences
f
in
{\mathcal{A}}

3.

\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(f)
for all left-upper corner embeddings
f
in
{\mathcal{A}}

4.

the comparison maps
\bigoplus_{i\in I}\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(A_{i})\to\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(\prod_{i\in I}A_{i})
for all finite families
(A_{i})_{i\in I}
in
{\mathcal{A}}
for which the product exists.

5.

the comparison maps
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(A_{i})\to\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}A_{i})
for all filtered diagrams
I\ni i\mapsto A_{i}\in{\mathcal{A}}
in
{\mathcal{A}}
for which the colimit exists.

Let

L_{0}:{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Sp})\leftrightarrows\mathrm{LOC}:\mathrm{incl}

be the left Bousfield localization with respect to these morphisms,
where
\mathrm{LOC}
denotes the full subcategory of
{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Sp})
of local objects.

In a second step
we form the left Bousfield localization

L_{1}:\mathrm{LOC}\leftrightarrows\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}}):\mathrm{incl}

which inverts the comparison morphisms

L_{0}\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(A)\to{\mathrm{Fib}}(L_{0}\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(B)\to L_{0}\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(C))

(2.2)

for all exact sequences
0\to A\to B\to C\to 0
in
{\mathcal{A}}
. The first step in this two-step process
ensures that
L_{0}(0)\simeq 0
, which is required for the comparison maps in (
2.2
) to be well-defined.
We then define

L:=L_{1}\circ L_{0}:{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}},\mathbf{Sp})\to\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}})

(2.3)

and set

\mathrm{e}_{{\mathcal{A}}}:=L\circ\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}})\ .

By construction, this functor has the desired universal property (
2.1
).

Note that the
E
-theory functor
\mathrm{e}_{{\mathcal{A}}}:{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}})
is uniquely determined (up to equivalence) by the
universal property (
2.1
).
∎

Remark 2.11
.

It follows from the construction in the proof of
Theorem

2.10
that
\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}})
is an object of
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
.
Furthermore, using the existence of the Toeplitz extension

0\to K\to{\mathcal{T}}\to C(S^{1})\to 0

in
\mathcal{N}
, one can show, following the arguments in
[
Bun24
]
, that
\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}})
satisfies Bott periodicity,
i.e, that there is an equivalence
\Omega^{2}\simeq{\mathtt{id}}
.
∎

Lemma 2.12
.

A morphism

f:{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
of
E
-theory contexts admits a unique colimit-preserving factoriation

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 14.04169pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{A}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.59547pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{f}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.33337pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-15.80556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{e}_{{\mathcal{A}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 45.33337pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{{\mathcal{B}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.87505pt\raise-15.80556pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.00694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{e}_{{\mathcal{B}}}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 51.87505pt\raise-23.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 14.50343pt\raise-25.11111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathrm{E}(f)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.04169pt\raise-31.61111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern 38.04169pt\raise-31.61111pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{B}})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

Proof.

By the universal property of
\mathrm{e}_{{\mathcal{A}}}
, it suffices to show that

\mathrm{e}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\circ f
is a homological functor. Since
f
is a morphism of
\mathcal{N}
-modules, it necessarily preserves
homotopy equivalences and left upper corner inclusions.
Furthermore, since
f
is
E
-admissible, it preserves exact sequences, finite products and filtered colimits. Consequently,
\mathrm{e}_{{\mathcal{B}}}\circ f
satisfies all conditions of
2.7
and is thus a homological functor. ∎

Theorem 2.13
.

There exists a 2-functor

\mathrm{E}:\mathcal{EC}\to\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}

(2.4)

equipped with a natural transformation
\mathrm{e}:{\mathtt{id}}\to\mathrm{E}
of
{\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty}
-valued functors making the square

\textstyle{\mathcal{EC}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{E}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{Cat}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty}}

commute.

Proof.

Recall that
\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}})
is obtained as a localization of
{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Sp})
at the set of morphisms

W_{{\mathcal{A}}}
inverted by
L
in (
2.3
). In light of
2.12

the
2
-functor

{\mathbf{Fun}}((-)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Sp}):\mathcal{EC}\to\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}^{L}

refines to a
2
-functor into the category of relative presentable stable categories

{\mathbf{Fun}}((-)^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Sp}):\mathcal{EC}\to\mathbf{Rel}\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}^{L}\ ,\quad{\mathcal{A}}\mapsto({\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{Sp}),W_{{\mathcal{A}}})\ .

Applying the localization functor

\mathbf{Rel}\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}^{L}\to\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}^{L}
yields the desired
2
-functor
\mathrm{E}
.
∎

2.3
Symmetric monoidal
E
-theory

We do not possess a symmetric monoidal category of
E
-theory contexts. If we model the symmetric monoidal category
{\mathbf{Cat}}
by an operad
{\mathbf{Cat}}^{\times}
as in
[
Lur
]
, then
\mathcal{EC}
can be viewed as a suboperad

\mathcal{EC}^{\otimes}
. However, the suboperad lacks sufficient cocartesian lifts to constitute a symmetric monoidal category.

Using the operad language, one can understand lax symmetric monoidal functors into
\mathcal{EC}
as operad maps into
{\mathbf{Cat}}^{\times}
that happen to take values in
\mathcal{EC}^{\otimes}
. The following
definitions provide a translation of this perspective into the language of ordinary category theory.

Consider a lax symmetric monoidal functor

{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{P}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}

from a symmetric monoidal category
(\mathcal{P},\otimes_{\mathcal{P}},1_{\mathcal{P}})
.

Definition 2.14
.

1.

We call
{\mathcal{A}}
a lax symmetric monoidal functor from
\mathcal{P}
to
E
-theory contexts if it satifies:

(a)

{\mathcal{A}}(1_{\mathcal{P}})
receives a symmetric monoidal functor from
\mathcal{N}
and
{\mathcal{A}}(p)
is pointed for every
p
in
P
.

(b)

For every morphism
p\to q
in
\mathcal{P}
the morphism

{\mathcal{A}}(p)\to{\mathcal{A}}(q)
is
E
-admissible.

(c)

For every
A
in
{\mathcal{A}}(p)
and every
q
in
\mathcal{P}
, the functor

A\boxtimes-:{\mathcal{A}}(q)\to{\mathcal{A}}(p\otimes_{\mathcal{P}}q)
is
E
-admissible.

2.

A natural transformation
{\mathcal{A}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
between two such functors is called a natural transformation between lax symmetric monoidal functors of
E
-theory contexts if the morphism
{\mathcal{A}}(p)\to{\mathcal{B}}(p)
is
E
-admissible for every
p
in
\mathcal{P}
.

Remark 2.15
.

In Condition
2.14
.
1a
we use that
{\mathcal{A}}(1_{\mathcal{P}})
is naturally a symmetric monoidal category.

Remark 2.16
.

If we take
\mathcal{P}=*
in
2.14
, then we refer to
{\mathcal{A}}
as a symmetric monoidal
E
-theory context.
∎

Remark 2.17
.

Let
{\mathcal{A}}
be a lax symmetric monoidal functor from
\mathcal{P}
to
E
-theory contexts as defined in
2.14
.
Condition
1a
of
2.14

ensures that the values
{\mathcal{A}}(p)
belong to
{\mathbf{Mod}}_{\mathcal{N}}({\mathbf{Cat}})_{*}
, while condition

1c
implies that
{\mathcal{A}}(p)
is indeed an
E
-theory context.
Consequently, by Condition
1b
, we obtain a functor

{\mathcal{A}}:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{EC}\ .

(2.5)

∎

By composing the functor from (
2.5
) with
\mathrm{E}
from (
2.4
), we obtain the functor

\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}}):\mathcal{P}\to\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}\ .

(2.6)

Theorem 2.18
.

If
{\mathcal{A}}
is a lax symmetric monoidal functor to
E
-theory contexts, then
the functor in (
2.6
) admits a canonical refinement to a lax symmetric monoidal functor.

Proof.

The composition of lax symmetric monoidal functors

{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}}(-),\mathbf{Sp}):\mathcal{P}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Cat}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathbf{Fun}}(-,\mathbf{Sp})}}{{\to}}\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{incl}}}{{\to}}{\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty}

lifts to a lax symmetric monoidal functor into the
\infty
-category of relative
\infty
-categories

({\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}}(-),\mathbf{Sp}),{\mathcal{W}}(-)):\mathcal{P}\to\mathbf{RelCat}_{\infty}\ ,

where
{\mathcal{W}}(p)
denotes the set of morphisms inverted by

L:{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}}(p),\mathbf{Sp})\to\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}}(p))
.
To this end we must check
for every
p,q
in
\mathcal{P}
, object
X
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}({\mathcal{A}}(p),\mathbf{Sp})
and
f
in
{\mathcal{W}}(p)
, that
the morphism
X\boxtimes f
is in
{\mathcal{W}}(p\times q)
.
Since the external product
\boxtimes
preserves colimits in each variable it suffices to show this for
objects of the form
X=\Sigma^{\infty}_{+}y(A)
with
A
in
{\mathcal{A}}(p)
, and the generators
f
of the localization
listed in the proof of
Theorem

2.10
. This follows immediately from the fact that the functor

A\boxtimes-
is
E
-admissible by
2.14
.
1c

We now compose with the symmetric monoidal localization functor

\mathbf{RelCat}_{\infty}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty}

to obtain a lax symmetric monoidal functor

\mathrm{E}({\mathcal{A}}):\mathcal{P}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty}\ .

Finally, we observe that this functor factorizes through a lax symmetric monoidal functor into
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
, as indicated
by the dotted arrow in the following diagram:
