A continuous map

f:X\to Y
between locally compact Hausdorff spaces induces the functor

f_{!}:XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,\quad(A,m)\mapsto(A,f_{!}m)\ ,

where

f_{!}m:C_{0}(Y)\otimes A\to C_{b}(X)\otimes A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\hat{m}}}{{\to}}A\ ,

with

\hat{m}
being the natural extension of
m
.

We have an inclusion

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}((A,m),(A^{\prime},m^{\prime}))\subseteq{\mathtt{Hom}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A,A^{\prime})\ .

(5.4)

∎

Remark 5.11
.

If
(A,m)
is in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, then the
A
-bilinear homomorphism

m:C_{0}(X)\otimes A\to A
satisfies

m(f\otimes m(g\otimes a))=m(fg\otimes a)\ .

(5.5)

To see this, we use an approximate unit
u
of
A
and

\displaystyle m(f\otimes m(g\otimes a))

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle m(f\otimes um(g\otimes a))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m(f\otimes u)m(g\otimes a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m(fg\otimes ua)

\displaystyle\sim

\displaystyle m(fg\otimes a)\ .

∎

In the following proposition, we describe filtered colimits, kernels, and quotients in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

in terms of the pairs
(A,m)
.
For the the second and third statements we consider the squares

\textstyle{(A,m)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(B,n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(C,r)}

(5.6)

and

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 15.17361pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.17361pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.17361pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-25.05556pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.17361pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.64063pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.28577pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 39.28577pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{C(X)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

(5.7)

Proposition 5.12
.

1.

A filtered colimit
(A,m)
of a system
(A_{i},m_{i})_{i\in I}
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is given by
A:=\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}A_{i}(X)
and the canonically induced
A
-bilinear multiplication map
m:C_{0}(X)\otimes A\to A
.

2.

A square (
5.6
) in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is cartesian
if and only if the square (
5.7
)
is cartesian in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

3.

A square (
5.6
) in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is cocartesian
if and only if the square (
5.7
)
is cocartesian in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

Proof.

Using that the maximal tensor product in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves filtered colimits, we define
m
by

C_{0}(X)\otimes A\cong C_{0}(X)\otimes\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}A_{i}\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}C_{0}(X)\otimes A_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(m_{i})_{i}}}{{\to}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}A_{i}\cong A\ .

A filtered colimit of surjective maps is again surjective. This shows that

(A,m)
is in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. We now show that it represents the colimit.
Let
(B,n)
be in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
We have

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A,B)\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in I}{\mathtt{Hom}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A_{i},B)\ .

Using (
5.4
), this bijection induces an injective map

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}((A,m),(B,n))\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in I}{\mathtt{Hom}}_{XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}((A_{i},m_{i}),(B,n))\ .

It remains to show that it is surjective.
Let
(f_{i})_{i\in I}
be a compatible system in the limit

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in I}{\mathtt{Hom}}_{XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}((A_{i},m_{i}),(B,n))

and
f
be its preimage in
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A,B)
. We must show that
f
actually belongs to the subset

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}((A,m),(B,n))
.
For
i
in
I
we consider the diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern A}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.09698pt\raise-89.22221pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 73.83466pt\raise-95.33331pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

We must show that the lower square commutes.
The outer square commutes by the definition of
f
.
By assumption, the upper square commutes for all
i
.
Hence, the composition of the lower two squares commutes for all
i
.
It follows from the universal property of
C_{0}(X)\otimes A
being the colimit of the system
(C_{0}(X)\otimes A_{i})_{i}

that the lower square commutes.
This finishes the proof of Assertion
1
.

We now show Assertions
2
and
3
.
We already know that fibre and cofibre sequences in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

are calculated pointwise.
If the square (
5.6
) is cartesian or cocartesian, then evaluating at
X
we see that the square (
5.7
) is cartesian or cocartesian, respectively.

For the converse assume that (
5.7
) is cartesian.
We must show that
A(U)={\mathtt{ker}}(B(U)\to C(U))
for all
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
.
We have
{\mathtt{ker}}(B(U)\to C(U))=A\cap B(U)
.
It remains to show that
A(U)=A\cap B(U)
.
It is clear that
A(U)\subseteq A\cap B(U)
.
For surjectivity,
consider
b
in
A\cap B(U)
. Then we can approximate
b
by elements
n(f\otimes b)
with
f
in
C_{0}(U)
.
We now note that
n(f\otimes b)\in A(U)
, so also
b\in A(U)
.

We now assume that (
5.7
) is cocartesian.
We must show that for
every
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
the projection

B(U)/A(U)\to C(A)
is an isomorphism.
It suffices to show that
A(U)
maps onto the kernel of the second map
B(U)\to C(U)
and that this map has a dense range.
For the latter, note that
the subspace of elements
r(g\otimes c)
with
c
in
C
and
g
in
C_{0}(U)
is dense in
C(U)
. For such elements,
we find
b
in
B
such that
b\mapsto c
. Then
n(g\otimes b)\in B(U)
and
n(g\otimes b)\mapsto r(g\otimes c)
.

Finally, assume that
b
is in
{\mathtt{ker}}(B(U)\to C(U)
. We can approximate

b
by elements of the form

n(f\otimes b)
with
f
in
C_{0}(U)
. These elements are in the image of
A(U)\to B(U)
. Hence,
the image of
A(U)\to B(U)
is the kernel of
B(U)\to C(U)
.
∎

5.3
Presentability of
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
for locally compact Hausdorff spaces

For a general pointed frame
P
we can define the
E
-theory context
P^{\mathrm{c}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
of continuous
P
-
C^{*}
-algebras,
but in contrast to
P^{?}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
for
?\in\{-,\mathrm{le}\}
, or also for
?=\mathrm{reg}
(assuming that
P
is stably locally compact), we have not yet shown that the category
P^{\mathrm{c}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is presentable. In this section, we show this presentability when
P=\mathrm{Open}(X)
for a locally compact Hausdorff space.

Remark 5.13
.

If
X
is a locally compact Hausdorff space, then
\mathrm{Open}(X)
is a stably locally compact frame. For
U,V
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
we write the way below relation as

V\Subset U
.
We have
V\Subset U

if and only if there exists a function
\phi
in
C_{c}(U)
taking values in
[0,1]
with

\phi_{|V}\equiv 1
. We call
\phi
a witness of the relation
V\Subset U
.

∎

The following notions will be used frequently below. Let
X
be a topological space and
A
be in
\mathrm{Open}(X)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

Definition 5.14
.

For a closed point
x
in
X
,
we call

A(x):=A(X)/A(X\setminus\{x\})

the fibre of
A
at
x
and
let
{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}:A\to A(x)
denote the projection map.

Theorem 5.15
.

For a locally compact Hausdorff space
X
,
we have an accessible left Bousfield localization

c:\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\leftrightarrows XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{incl}

(5.8)

and
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is presentable.

Proof.

We construct the adjunction in (
5.8
) explicitly by describing the functor
c
and the unit and counit. The functor
c
is actually the restriction of a functor

c:\mathrm{Open}(X)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

(5.9)

to the subcategory
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Let
A
be in
\mathrm{Open}(X)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. We use
A
also to denote the
C^{*}
-algebra
A(X)
.
Then we can form the tensor product
C_{0}(X)\otimes A
. We have a canonical isomorphism

C_{0}(X)\otimes A\cong C_{0}(X,A)
. For every
x
in
X
, we consider the evaluation

e_{x}:C_{0}(X,A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\phi\mapsto\phi(x)}}{{\to}}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a\mapsto{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a)}}{{\to}}A(x)\ .

We let
I
be the kernel of

(e_{x})_{x\in X}:C_{0}(X)\otimes A\to\prod_{x\in X}A(x)\ .

The elements of
I
are thus functions
\phi
in
C_{0}(X,A)
with
\phi(x)\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
for every
x
in
X
.
We then define the underlying
C^{*}
-algebra of
c(A)
by

c(A):=(C_{0}(X)\otimes A)/I\ .

We have a map of exact sequences

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{0}(X)\otimes I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{0}(X)\otimes C_{0}(X)\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{0}(X)\otimes c(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m^{\prime}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{0}(X)\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{c(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

where the middle vertical map is induced by
C_{0}(X)\otimes C_{0}(X)\cong C_{0}(X\times X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{diag}}^{*}}}{{\to}}C_{0}(X)
.
An element of
C_{0}(X)\otimes I
can be considered as a function
\phi:X\times X\to A
such that

\phi(x,y)\in A(X\setminus\{y\})
for every
x,y
in
X
.
The middle vertical map sends this function to
x\mapsto\phi(x,x):X\to A
, which obviously belongs to
I
.
Hence the left vertical map is well-defined.
We conclude the existence of the right vertical map, which is surjective
since the middle map is surjective. It is the
c(A)
-bilinear multiplication map
witnessing
(c(A),m^{\prime})
as an object of
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

We now construct the counit

\eta:c\circ\mathrm{incl}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}{\mathtt{id}}:XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ .

Lemma 5.16
.

If
A
is in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, then

{\mathtt{ev}}:=({\mathtt{ev}}_{x})_{x\in X}:A\to\prod_{x\in X}A(x)

(5.10)

is injective.

Proof.

Let
\tilde{\psi}:\mathrm{Prim}(A)\to X
be the map from
5.6
corresponding to the structure of
A
and
m:C_{0}(X)\otimes A\to A
the structure homomorphism from
5.4
.
For every
\rho
in
\mathrm{Prim}(A)
we have
A(X\setminus\{\tilde{\psi}(\rho)\})\subseteq{\mathtt{ker}}(\rho)
.
To see this, note that, by (
5.3
), we must show that

\rho(m(f\otimes a))=0
for all
a
in
A(X)
and
f
in
C_{0}(X\setminus\{\tilde{\psi}(\rho)\})
.
We have
\rho(m(f\otimes a))=f(\tilde{\psi}(\rho))\rho(a)=0
since
f(\tilde{\psi}(\rho))=0
.
Hence we get a homomorphism
A(\tilde{\psi}(\rho))\to A(X)/{\mathtt{ker}}(\rho)
.
If
({\mathtt{ev}}_{x})_{x\in X}(a)=0
, then also
0=([a]_{A(X)/{\mathtt{ker}}(\rho)})_{\rho\in\mathrm{Prim}(A(X))}
.
This implies, by
5.5
.
2
, that
a=0
.
∎

Assume that
A
is in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. By
5.4

we have the structure morphism

m:C_{0}(X)\otimes A\to A\ .

Lemma 5.17
.

The kernel of
m
is precisely the ideal
I
.

Proof.

Using
5.16
we can conclude that
the kernel of
m
is precisely the kernel of

e:C_{0}(X)\otimes A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\to}}A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle({\mathtt{ev}}_{x})_{x\in X}}}{{\to}}\prod_{x\in X}A(x)\ .

We now show that the following square

\textstyle{C_{0}(X)\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{m}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{({\mathtt{ev}}_{x})_{x\in X}}
\textstyle{C_{0}(X,A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi\mapsto(\phi(x))_{x\in X}}
\textstyle{\prod_{x\in X}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\prod_{x\in X}{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}}
\textstyle{\prod_{x\in X}A(x)}

commutes.
It suffices to check commutativity on elements of the form
f\otimes a
in
C_{0}(X)\otimes A
, and this follows from

{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(m(f\otimes a))=f(x){\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a)={\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(f(x)a)

(5.11)

in
A(x)
for every
x
in
X
.
The ideal
I
is by definition the kernel of the down-right composition, which coincides
with the kernel of the right-down composition. This finishes the verification that
I
is the kernel of
m
.∎

By
5.17
for
A
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
we have a canonical isomorphism

\eta_{A}:c\circ\mathrm{incl}(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}A\ ,\qquad[f\otimes a]\mapsto m(f\otimes a)

of plain
C^{*}
-algebras.
We must check that this is a morphism in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. To this end, we must check that the square

\textstyle{C_{0}(X)\otimes c\circ\mathrm{incl}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C_{0}(X)\otimes A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{c\circ\mathrm{incl}(A)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A}

commutes, see
5.10
.
Let
f
be in
C_{0}(X)
and
[g\otimes a]
be in
c\circ\mathrm{incl}(A)
. Then the square gives

\textstyle{f\otimes[g\otimes a]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f\otimes m(g\otimes a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[fg\otimes a]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{m(fg\otimes a)=m(f\otimes m(g\otimes a))}

The equality at the lower corner is (
5.5
).

We now define the unit

\epsilon:{\mathtt{id}}\to\mathrm{incl}\circ c

as follows.
Let
A
be in
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. Then the component at
A
of the unit of the adjunction (
3.7
) is an isomorphism

A\cong R(A)\ ,

where we consider
R
as defined in
\mathrm{Open}(X)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and omit to write inclusions explicitly.
Therefore for every

U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
we have

\overline{\bigcup_{V\Subset U}A(V)}=A(U)\ .

For

a
in
A(U)

we must define
\epsilon_{A}(a)
in
c(A)(U)
.
Assume first that
a\in\bigcup_{V\Subset U}A(V)
.
Then
a\in A(V)
for some

V\Subset U
witnessed by
\phi
.
In this case, we define
\eta_{A}(a):=[\phi\otimes a]
in
c(A)
.
This is independent of the choice of
\phi
and multiplicative. To see independence,
let
\phi^{\prime}
be a second choice. Then for every
x
in
X
, we have

{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}((\phi-\phi^{\prime})a)\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
. Indeed,

{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}((\phi-\phi^{\prime})a)=0
for
x
in
V
and
{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}((\phi-\phi^{\prime})a)\in A(V)
for
x\not\in V
and hence
V\subseteq X\setminus\{x\}
.
In a similar manner, we show that this describes a
*
-algebra homomorphism. We then extend this map to all of
A(U)
by continuity.

We next check the triangle identities.
We argue that for
A
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the composition

c(A)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle c(\epsilon_{A})}}{{\to}}c(\mathrm{incl}(c(A)))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eta_{c(A)}}}{{\to}}c(A)

is the identity.
Let
[\psi\otimes a]
be in
c(A)(U)
with

a
in
A(V)
for

V\Subset U
witnessed by
\phi
, and
\psi\in C_{0}(U)
.
The elements of this form generate
c(A)(U)
.
The composition sends this to
[\psi\phi\otimes a]
, but

[\psi\phi\otimes a]=[\psi\otimes a]
.

Furthermore, for
B
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
we show that the composition

\mathrm{incl}(B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\epsilon_{\mathrm{incl}(B)}}}{{\to}}\mathrm{incl}(c(\mathrm{incl}(B)))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{incl}(\eta_{B})}}{{\to}}\mathrm{incl}(B)

is the identity. Note that
\mathrm{incl}(B)(U)
is generated by

b
in
B(V)
for

V\Subset U
witnessed by
\phi
. The composition sends

b
to
m(\phi\otimes b)
. We have

{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(m(\phi\otimes b))=\phi(x){\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(b)={\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(b)
since
\phi(x)=1
for
x
in
V
and

{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(b)=0
for
x\not\in V
.
This finishes the verification that (
5.8
) is a left Bousfield localization.

We already know from a combination of
3.23
and
3.19
that
\mathrm{incl}
preserves filtered colimits. Since
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is an accessible left Bousfield localization
of
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, which is presentable by
Theorem

3.17
, we conclude that
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is also presentable.
∎

For later use, we record the following corollary of the proof of
Theorem

5.15
.

Corollary 5.18
.

If
X
is a locally compact Hausdorff space and
(A,m)
is in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, then
we have an exact sequence

0\to I\to C_{0}(X)\otimes A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m}}{{\to}}A\to 0

where the ideal
I
consists precisely of the functions
\phi
in
C_{0}(X,A)\cong C_{0}(X)\otimes A
with

\phi(x)\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
for all
x
in
X
.

5.4
Fell bundles

Recall that for a locally compact Hausdorff space, we can equivalently describe objects of
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
as
pairs
(A,A(-))
of a
C^{*}
-algebra equippped with a frame morphism
\mathrm{Open}(X)\to I(A)
, or as pairs
(A,m)
consisting of a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
equipped with a surjective homomorphism
C_{0}(X)\otimes A\to A
. In this section, we provide a third alternative picture of
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
in terms of upper semicontinuous
C^{*}
-bundles or Fell bundles (see
5.25
below). This equivalence is
well-known in the
C^{*}
-literature and is essentially due to
[
Nil96
]
.
As the notation and some of the details are relevant for later constructions, we
reproduce the arguments for completeness.
We apply the Fell-bundle picture in order to describe the contravariant functorality

\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}\ ,\quad X\mapsto XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,\quad f\mapsto f^{*}\ .

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space, consider
A
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, and recall
5.14
. The
C^{*}
-algebra
C_{b}(X)
acts by multipliers on the
C^{*}
-algebra
\prod_{x\in X}A(x)
.
We have a canonical map

{\mathtt{ev}}:A\to\prod_{x\in X}A(x)\ ,\quad a\mapsto{\mathtt{ev}}(a):=({\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a))_{x\in X}\ .

The elements in the product are called sections, and for

a
in
A
, we call
{\mathtt{ev}}(a)
the constant section associated to
a
.

Lemma 5.19
.

Assume that
A
belongs to
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. Then the following assertions are equivalent:

1.

We have
A\in XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

2.

The map

{\mathtt{ev}}:A\to\prod_{x\in X}A(x)
is injective, its image is preserved by
multiplication with elements of
C_{0}(X)
, and for every
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
the ideal

{\mathtt{ev}}(A(U))
is precisely the ideal generated by products with
C_{0}(U)
.

Proof.

If
A
is in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
corresponding to a pair
(A,m)
as in
5.4
, then

{\mathtt{ev}}
is injective by
5.16
.
The algebra
A
is identified by
{\mathtt{ev}}
with the algebra of constant sections.
In view of
{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(m(f\otimes a))=f(x){\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a)
(see (
5.11
))
and the surjectivity of
m

the image of
{\mathtt{ev}}
is also the algebra generated by sections of the form
f{\mathtt{ev}}(a)
for
f
in
C_{0}(X)
and
a
in
A
. In particular, it is preserved by the multiplication by
C_{0}(X)
.
Finally, for every
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
, the ideal
{\mathtt{ev}}(A(U))
is precisely the ideal generated by products with
C_{0}(U)
.
Hence Assertion
1
implies Assertion
2
.

Conversely, assume Assertion
2
. The sub-
C^{*}
-algebra of
\prod_{x\in X}A(x)

generated by products of constant sections with elements of
C_{0}(X)

is precisely
c(A)
considered in
Theorem

5.15
. Let
m:C_{0}(X)\otimes c(A)\to c(A)
denote the obvious multiplication map. Then
(c(A),m)
belongs to

XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

By assumption, the map
{\mathtt{ev}}
induces an injective map
A\to c(A)
of
C^{*}
-algebras. But this map is also surjective by the definition of
c(A)
.
The conditions in
2
imply that
A\to c(A)
is actually an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Open}(X)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, and hence in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Hence Assertion
2
implies Assertion
1
.
∎

Construction 5.20
.

Assume that
(A_{x})_{x\in X}
is a family of
C^{*}
-algebras indexed by the points of a locally compact Hausdorff space
X
. We consider a subalgebra
A
of
\prod_{x\in X}A_{x}
. We assume that
A
is preserved by the multiplication with elements of
C_{0}(X)
and that the
A
-bilinear multiplication map
m:C_{0}(X)\otimes A\to A
is surjective.
For
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
, we define
A(U)
as the subalgebra generated by products of
A
with elements of
C_{0}(U)
. This is a closed
*
-ideal. We let
\pi_{x}:A\to A_{x}
denote the restriction of the projection to the factor with index
x
.
∎

Lemma 5.21
.

In the situation of
5.20
, we have the following assertions.

1.

A
with the structure
\mathrm{Open}(X)\ni U\mapsto A(U)\in I(A)
belongs to
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

2.

For every
x
in
X
and
\phi
in
C_{0}(X)\otimes A\cong C_{0}(X,A)
we have

\pi_{x}(m(\phi))=\pi_{x}(\phi(x))\ .

3.

For every
a
in
A
, the function
X\ni x\mapsto\|\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A(x)}
vanishes at
\infty
.

Proof.

The Assertion
1
is
precisely the description of the functor
\mathrm{Open}(X)\to I(A)
obtained from the pair
(A,m)
via the equivalence
asserted in

5.4
.

It suffices to check the identity claimed in Assertion
2
on elements of the form
\kappa\otimes a
for
\kappa
in
C_{0}(X)
and
a
in
A
. By the definition of the multiplication map, we have

\pi_{x}(m(\kappa\otimes a))=\kappa(x)\pi_{x}(a)\ .

On the other hand,

\pi_{x}((\kappa\otimes a)(x))=\pi_{x}(\kappa(x)a)=\kappa(x)\pi_{x}(a)\ .

We now show Assertion
3
.
The function
x\mapsto\|\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A_{x}}
is bounded by
\|a\|_{A}
.
We can approximate
a
by elements of the form
m(f\otimes a)
for
f\in C_{0}(X)
. Then by Assertion
1

x\mapsto\|\pi_{x}(m(f\otimes a))\|_{A_{x}}=|f(x)|\|\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A_{x}}

vanishes at
\infty
. It follows that the original function also vanishes at
\infty
.
∎

The algebra
A
described in
5.20
is an object of
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, but in this generality
the algebras
A_{x}
are not necessarily the fibres defined in
5.14
.
The following result provides a sufficient criterion for this property and clarifies the role of the condition that the functions

x\mapsto\|\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A_{x}}
are upper semi-continuous for all
a
in
A
.

Proposition 5.22
.

In the situation of
5.20
the following assertions are equivalent:

1.

For every
x
in
X
, the map
\pi_{x}
induces an isomorphism
A(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}A_{x}
.

2.

For every
a
in
A
, the function
x\mapsto\|\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A_{x}}
is upper semi-continuous and
A\to A_{x}
is surjective for all
x\in X
.

Proof.

By the definition of
A(x)
, we have an exact sequence

0\to A(X\setminus\{x\})\to A\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}}}{{\to}}A(x)\to 0\ .

Furthermore,
A(X\setminus\{x\})\subseteq{\mathtt{ker}}(\pi_{x})
since it is generated by elements
m(\kappa\otimes a)
with
\kappa(x)=0
, and using
5.21
.
2
, we have
\pi_{x}(m(\kappa\otimes a))=\kappa(x)\pi_{x}(a)=0
.
Therefore, the map
A(x)\to A_{x}
induced by
\pi_{x}
is well-defined.

We show that Assertion
1
implies Assertion
2
.
We consider
a
in
A
and must show that
x\mapsto\|\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A_{x}}
is upper semi-continuous.
Since the multiplication map
m:C_{0}(X)\otimes A\to A
is surjective there exists
\phi
in
C_{0}(X,A)
such that
m(\phi)=a
. By
5.21
.
2

for every
x
in
X
,
we have

\pi_{x}(a)=\pi_{x}(m(\phi))=\pi_{x}(\phi(x))\ .

We now use that
A_{x}\cong A(x)\cong A/A(X\setminus\{x\})
.
Given
\epsilon
in
(0,\infty)
and
x_{0}
in
X
, we find
b
in
A(X\setminus\{x_{0}\})
such that

\|\phi(x_{0})+b\|_{A}\leq\|\pi_{x_{0}}(a)\|_{A_{x_{0}}}+\epsilon/3\ .

Since
A(X\setminus\{x_{0}\})
is the image of the multiplication map
C_{0}(X\setminus\{x_{0}\})\otimes A\to A
,
we can find
\beta
in
C_{0}(X\setminus\{x_{0}\})\otimes A
such that

m(\beta)=b
. We can find an open neighborhood
V
of
x_{0}
and
\kappa
in
C_{0}(X\setminus\bar{V})\otimes A
such that
\|\beta-\kappa\beta\|\leq\epsilon/3
. We have
\|b-m(\kappa\beta)\|\leq\epsilon/3
and
m(\kappa\beta)\in A(X\setminus\bar{V})
. Now let
\chi
be in
C_{0}(V)
such that
\chi(x_{0})=1
. Then
\chi\otimes m(\kappa\beta)\in{\mathtt{ker}}(m)
since
\pi_{x}(m(\chi\otimes\kappa\beta))=\chi(x)\pi_{x}(\kappa\beta)=0
for all
x
in
X
. Indeed,
if
\chi(x)\not=0
, then
x\in V
and
\pi_{x}(\kappa\beta)=0
since
\kappa\beta\in A(X\setminus\bar{V})\subseteq A(X\setminus\{x\})
.

We can replace
\phi
by

\phi+\chi\otimes m(\kappa\beta)
. Since
m(\chi\otimes m(\kappa\beta))=0
we
still have
m(\phi)=a
, but since
\chi(x_{0})m(\kappa\beta)=m(\kappa\beta)
we now have

\|\phi(x_{0})\|_{A}\leq\|\pi_{x_{0}}(a)\|_{A_{x_{0}}}+2\epsilon/3\ .

By the continuity of
y\mapsto\|\phi(y)\|_{A}
there exists a neighborhood
U
of
x_{0}
such that for all
x
in
U
, we have

\|\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A_{x}}\leq\|\phi(x)\|_{A}\leq\|\phi(x_{0})\|_{A}+\epsilon/3\leq\|\pi_{x_{0}}(a)\|_{A_{x_{0}}}+\epsilon\ .

This finishes the verification that
x\mapsto\|\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A_{x}}
is upper semi-continuous.

We now show that Assertion
2
implies Assertion
1
.
We must show that
A(X\setminus\{x_{0}\})={\mathtt{ker}}(\pi_{x_{0}})
for every
x_{0}
in
X
.
As seen at the beginning of this proof, we have
A(X\setminus\{x_{0}\})\subseteq{\mathtt{ker}}(\pi_{x_{0}})
. In order to show the converse
inclusion, consider
a
in
{\mathtt{ker}}(\pi_{x_{0}})
. Then
\|\pi_{x_{0}}(a)\|_{A_{x_{0}}}=0
. Fix
\epsilon
in
(0,\infty)
. Since

x\mapsto\|\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A_{x}}
is upper semi-continuous and vanishes at
\infty
by
5.21
.
3
, we can find
\kappa
in
C_{0}(X\setminus\{x\})
such that

\|m(\kappa\otimes a)-a\|_{A}=\sup_{x\in X}\|\kappa(x)\pi_{x}(a)-\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A_{x}}\leq\epsilon\ .

But
m(\kappa\otimes a)\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
. Since
\epsilon
can be chosen arbitrarily small and the ideal
A(X\setminus\{x\})
is closed, we conclude that
a\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
.
∎

Remark 5.23
.

In the situation of
5.20
, assume that
A
and
A^{\prime}
are subalgebras of

\prod_{x\in X}A_{x}
preserved by
C_{0}(X)
with a surjective multiplication map.

Lemma 5.24

(
[
LG99
, Prop. 3.1]
)
.

If
A
and
A^{\prime}
satisfy the equivalent conditions in
5.22
, and if

A\subseteq A^{\prime}
, then
A=A^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Let
a^{\prime}
be in
A^{\prime}
.
We must show that
a^{\prime}\in A
.
We can approximate
a^{\prime}
by elements satisfying
a^{\prime}=\kappa a^{\prime}
for some
\kappa
in
C_{c}(X)
. Then
{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a^{\prime})=0
for
x\not\in{\mathtt{supp}}(\kappa)
.
For every
x
in
X
, we can find
a_{x}
in
A
such that
{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a_{x})={\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a^{\prime})
. Fix
\epsilon
in
(0,\infty)
.
Then there exists a neighborhood
V_{x}
of
x
such that

\|{\mathtt{ev}}_{y}(a^{\prime}-a)\|_{A_{y}}\leq\epsilon
for all
y
in
V_{x}
.
We choose a partition of unity
(\chi_{x})_{x\in X}
subordinated to the covering
(V_{x})_{x\in\sup(\kappa)}
.
We choose a finite family
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
of points in
X
such that

{\mathtt{supp}}(\kappa)\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in I}V_{i}
.
We then consider the element

\sum_{i\in I}\chi_{x_{i}}a_{x_{i}}

in
A
.
We have

\|\sum_{i\in I}\chi_{x_{i}}a_{x_{i}}-a^{\prime}\|\leq\sup_{y\in X}\|{\mathtt{ev}}_{y}(\sum_{i\in I}\chi_{x_{i}}(a_{x_{i}}-a^{\prime}))\|_{A_{y}}\leq\sum_{i\in I}\chi_{x_{i}}(x)\sup_{y\in V_{x_{i}}}\|{\mathtt{ev}}_{y}(a_{x})-a^{\prime})\|_{A_{y}}\leq\epsilon\ .

Since
\epsilon
is arbitrary, we conclude that
a^{\prime}\in A
.
∎

∎

Combining the results above, we arrive at our third characterization of
X
-
C^{*}
-algebras in terms of Fell bundles (compare with
[
Nil96
, Thm. 2.3]
).

Corollary 5.25
.

For a locally compact Hausdorff space and a family
(A_{x})_{x\in X}
of
C^{*}
-algebras,
the following data are equivalent:

1.

An object
A
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
with
A_{x}\cong A(x)
for all
x
in
X
.

2.

A subalgebra

A\subseteq\prod_{x\in X}A_{x}
that is invariant under and generated by multiplication by functions in
C_{0}(X)

and such that the map
x\mapsto\|\pi_{x}(a)\|_{A_{x}}
is upper semi-continuous for every
a
in
A
and
\pi_{x}:A\to A_{x}
is surjective for every
x
in
X
.

Proof.

Given an object
A
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
with
A_{x}\cong A(x)
for all
x
in
X
, we can view

A
as the subalgebra of constant sections of
\prod_{x\in X}A_{x}
.

Conversely, let
A
be in as in Assertion
2
. Then
A
is in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
A_{x}\cong A(x)
for all
x
in
X
.
∎

5.5
The
f^{*}
-functoriality and adjunctions

We first describe the covariant functoriality of
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
in the Fell bundle picture.
Let
f:X\to Y
be a continuous map between locally compact Hausdorff spaces, and
A
be in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

Lemma 5.26
.

The object
f_{!}A
in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
can be identified with the subalgebra
A
of constant sections of

\prod_{x\in X}A(x)\cong\prod_{y\in Y}\left(\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}A(x)\right)

with the structure map
C_{0}(Y)\otimes f_{!}A\to A
determined by this decomposition of the product.

Proof.

This follows from unfolding definitions.
∎

Note that here we use the homomorphism

f^{*}:C_{0}(Y)\to C_{b}(X)
and that
C_{b}(X)
acts on
A
by central multipliers.

Corollary 5.27
.

The fibre
(f_{!}A)(y)
is precisely the subalgebra of
\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}A(x)
generated by the constant sections

f^{-1}(\{y\})\ni x\mapsto{\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a)
for
a
in
A
.

Now let
f:X\to Y
be a map, and
A
be in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
We use the Fell bundle picture from
5.25
in order to construct

f^{*}A
such that
(f^{*}A)(x)=A(f(x))
for all
x
in
X
.

Definition 5.28
.

We define

f^{*}A
as the subalgebra of

\prod_{x\in X}A(f(x))
generated by the products of constant sections with elements of
C_{0}(X)
.

This definition is justified as follows. Recall
that a constant section is a section of the form
x\mapsto{\mathtt{ev}}_{f(x)}(a)
for
a
in
A
.
By
5.21
.
1
we get the structure of
f^{*}A
as an object of
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Observe that
x\mapsto\|{\mathtt{ev}}_{f(x)}(a)\|_{A(f(x))}
is upper semi-continuous. Furthermore,
given
x
and
b
in
A(f(x))
there exists
a
in
A
such that
{\mathtt{ev}}_{f(x)}(a)=b
.
Hence
f^{*}A\to A(f(x))
is surjective. In view of
5.22
we get

(f^{*}A)(x)\cong A(f(x))\ .

(5.12)

Thus,
f^{*}A
has the desired fibres.

Remark 5.29
.

We can express
f^{*}A
in terms of the balanced tensor product

f^{*}A\cong C_{0}(X)\otimes_{C_{0}(Y)}A\ ,

however, the above definition
is more amenable to verifying the functoriality, providing the coherences below, and describing the lax symmetric monoidal
structure introduced in
Section

5.6
.

Assume that
g:Y\to Z
is a second map.

Lemma 5.30
.

We have a canonical isomorphism
\alpha_{g,f}:(g\circ f)^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}f^{*}g^{*}
.

Proof.

Let
A
be in
ZC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. Then

g^{*}A
is by definition the subalgebra of
\prod_{y\in Y}A(g(y))
generated by products of constant sections with elements of
C_{0}(Y)
. Furthermore,

f^{*}g^{*}A
is by definition the subalgebra of

\prod_{x\in X}(g^{*}A)(f(x))
generated by
products of constant sections associated to elements of
g^{*}A
with functions in
C_{0}(X)
.
For every
x
in
X
by (
5.12
)
we have the isomorphism

(g^{*}A)(f(x))\cong A(g(f(x))\ .

(5.13)

Thus, we can identify

f^{*}g^{*}A
with a subalgebra of

\prod_{x\in X}A(g(f(x)))

generated by the products of constant sections associated to elements of
A

with products of the form
f^{*}h\cdot l
for
l
in
C_{0}(X)
and
h
in
C_{0}(Y)
.
But this is the same as the subalgebra generated by the products of constant sections associated to elements of
A
with elements of
C_{0}(X)
.
The latter is precisely the definition of
(g\circ f)^{*}A
.
Consequently,
\alpha_{g,f}
is the identification of the corresponding subalgebras
of
\prod_{x\in X}(g^{*}A)(f(x))
and
\prod_{x\in X}A(g(f(x)))
, where these products are identified by the family of isomorphisms
(
5.13
) for all
x
in
X
.
∎

Note that
{\mathbf{Cat}}
is a
2
-category. Since we implicitly always work with
\infty
-categories, a
functor from a
1
-category to
{\mathbf{Cat}}
is what is classically called a pseudofunctor.

Proposition 5.31
.

We have constructed a functor

\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}\ ,\quad X\mapsto C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,\quad f\mapsto f^{*}\ .

Proof.

By
5.30

for every composable pair
f,g
of morphisms in
\mathrm{LCH}
we have defined an isomorphism

\alpha_{g,f}:f^{*}\circ g^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}(g\circ f)^{*}
.
These isomorphisms satisfy the associator relation expressed by the commutativity of the diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 19.91728pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&\cr&&\cr&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ ,

(5.14)

which is checked by unfolding definitions.
We also have
{\mathtt{id}}^{*}={\mathtt{id}}
and the compatibility of unit relations holds.
∎

The following result extends the adjunction in
4.11
from closed embeddings to proper maps between locally compact Hausdorff spaces.

Lemma 5.32
.

If
f:X\to Y
is a proper map between locally compact Hausdorff spaces, then we have an adjunction

f^{*}:YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\leftrightarrows XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:f_{!}\ .

(5.15)

If
f
is injective, then
f^{*}f_{!}\cong{\mathtt{id}}
.

Proof.

We describe the unit and counit and then check the triangle identities.
For
A
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
we consider the map

\prod_{x\in X}\prod_{x^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(\{x\})}A(x^{\prime})\to\prod_{x\in X}A(x)

given by the evaluation at
x^{\prime}=x
. It restricts to the counit

f^{*}f_{!}A\to A
.
To this end, we observe that it preserves constant sections and is
C_{0}(X)
-linear.
If
f
is injective, then it is an isomorphism.

For
B
in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
we consider the map

\ \prod_{y\in B}B(y)\to\prod_{y\in Y}\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}B(y)

given by the obvious diagonal map. Since
f
is proper, it sends
C_{0}(Y)
to
C_{0}(X)
via pull-back. Using this,
we see that the map restricts to

B\to f_{!}f^{*}B\ .

The triangle identities are straightforward.
∎

Remark 5.33
.

It is an interesting question whether, in the statement of
5.32
, one can
show the existence of a left-adjoint of
f_{!}
under weaker conditions on
X
.
But one can consider other assumptions on
Y
and
f
(see
5.53
).
∎

Lemma 5.34
.

If
f:X\to Y
is an open inclusion, then we have a right Bousfield localization

f_{!}:XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\leftrightarrows YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:f^{*}\ .

Proof.

We know by

4.10
that
f_{!}
has a right-adjoint.
The point of the lemma is that this right-adjoint is given by the functor from
5.31
.
To this end we write down the unit and counit of the adjunction explicitly.
The unit
A\to f^{*}f_{!}A
for
A
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is
the isomorphism obtained by restricting the isomorphism

\prod_{x\in X}A(x)\to\prod_{x\in X}\prod_{y\in f^{-1}(\{f(x)\}}A(x)

to
A
. The counit
f_{!}f^{*}B\to B
for
B
in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the inclusion of a subproduct obtained by restricting

\prod_{y\in Y}\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}B(y)\to\prod_{y\in Y}B(y)

to
B
. Note that
f^{-1}(\{y\})
is either empty or consists of precisely one point.
The triangle identities are straightforward to check.
∎

For the proof of
Theorem

5.2
we must verify various base-change properties relating the contravariant and covariant functorialities.
We consider a cartesian diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\textstyle{W}

(5.16)

in
\mathrm{LCH}
.

Lemma 5.35
.

We have a canonical isomorphism

j_{!}g^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}f^{*}i_{!}:YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to ZC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ .

(5.17)

Proof.

Let
A
be in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
We consider the map

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in Z}\prod_{x\in j^{-1}(\{z\})}A(g(x))

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in Z}\prod_{x\in j^{-1}(\{z\})}\prod_{y\in i^{-1}(\{i(g(x))\}}A(g(x))

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in Z}\prod_{x\in j^{-1}(\{z\})}\prod_{y\in i^{-1}(\{f(z)\}}A(y)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in Z}\prod_{y\in i^{-1}(\{f(z)\})}A(y)

The first map is the obvious diagonal.
The component of the second map at
z
is the restriction along
y\mapsto(x_{y},y)
, where

y
is in
i^{-1}(\{f(z)\})
and
x_{y}
is the unique point in
X
with
g(x_{y})=y
and
j(x_{y})=z
. Here we use that the square (
5.16
) is cartesian.
For every
z
the map
y\mapsto x_{y}
induces an identification of the index sets
of the inner products in the domain and target. Under this identification,
the whole composition becomes the identity.
One now checks that
the composition restricts to an isomorphism
in
ZC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
To this end we observe that it preserves constant sections and is compatible with the multiplication
by
C_{0}(Z)
.
∎

Remark 5.36
.

The isomorphism found in
5.35
is not yet determined by
adjunction data. But the point of the following

5.37
and
5.38
is that this isomorphism is the Beck-Chevalley morphism in the cases where the adjunction data exists.
∎

Lemma 5.37
.

Consider a cartesian square (
5.16
) and assume that
i
and hence
j
are open embeddings.
Then the square

\textstyle{WC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}
\textstyle{ZC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\textstyle{XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}

is horizontally left-adjoinable.

Proof.

One checks, using the proof of
5.34
, that the isomorphism in (
5.17
) is precisely
the composition of left-adjoints

j_{!}g^{*}\to j_{!}g^{*}i^{*}i_{!}\cong j_{!}j^{*}f^{*}i_{!}\to f^{*}i_{!}

induced by the respective unit and counit.
∎

We consider a cartesian diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{W}

(5.18)

in
\mathrm{LCH}
.

Lemma 5.38
.

Assume that
p,q
in the cartesian square (
5.18
) are proper. Then
the square

\textstyle{WC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\textstyle{ZC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{*}}
\textstyle{YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}
\textstyle{XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}

(5.19)

is vertically right-adjoinable

Proof.

We must show that
the Beck-Chevalley canonical
transformation

f^{*}p_{!}\to q_{!}q^{*}f^{*}p_{!}\cong q_{!}g^{*}p^{*}p_{!}\to q_{!}g^{*}:YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to ZC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

(5.20)

is an isomorphism.
For
A
in
ZC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
this map is the restriction of

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in Z}\prod_{y\in p^{-1}(\{f(z)\})}A(y)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in Z}\prod_{x\in q^{-1}(\{z\})}\prod_{y\in p^{-1}(\{f(z)\})}A(y)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in Z}\prod_{x\in q^{-1}(\{z\})}\prod_{x^{\prime}\in q^{-1}(\{z\})}A(g(x^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\prod_{z\in Z}\prod_{x\in q^{-1}(\{z\})}A(g(x))

where the first map is the obvious diagonal map and the second map is the evaluation at
x=x^{\prime}
.
Since (
5.18
) is cartesian, for a fixed
z
in
Z
there is a bijection

p^{-1}(\{f(z)\})\ni y\mapsto x_{y}\in q^{-1}(z)
uniquely determined by the condition
g(x_{y})=y
.
Using this bijection, we can identify, for each
z
in
Z
, the inner products in the domain and target.
Under this identification, the composition becomes the identity.
We observe that it preserves constant sections and is compatible with the multiplication by
C_{0}(Z)
.
Consequently, the isomorphism restricts to the isomorphism (
5.20
).
∎

Remark 5.39
.

The proof of
5.38

shows that (
5.20
) is the inverse of (
5.17
).
∎

5.6
Symmetric monoidal structure

In this section, we equip the functor

(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}\ ,\quad X\mapsto XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,\quad f\mapsto f^{*}

(5.21)

from
5.31

with a lax symmetric monoidal structure. Part of the lax symmetric monoidal structure is, for every two locally compact Hausdorff spaces
X
and
Y
, the datum of a bifunctor

-\boxtimes-:XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\times YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to(X\times Y)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ .

This bifunctor has already been considered in
[
Bla95
]
,
[
EW98
]
,
[
Pop04
]
.
Since
{\mathbf{Cat}}
is a
2
-category, defining a lax symmetric monoidal functor to
{\mathbf{Cat}}
requires providing a considerable amount of additional data and checking many coherences. The main point of this section is to
provide this data completely and to check the necessary coherences, thereby keeping the
necessary work at a reasonable amount. We will furthermore check the projection formulas, which go into the proof
of
Theorem

5.2
stating that
(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is a three-functor formalism.

Proposition 5.40
.

The
functor in
(
5.21
)
canonically extends to a lax symmetric monoidal functor.

Proof.

We start with the composition of lax symmetric monoidal functors

\Phi:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Set}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}\ ,\qquad X\to X^{\delta}\to\prod_{X^{\delta}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,

where
the first functor sends
X
to its underlying set
X^{\delta}
, and
the second functor is the restriction of the lax symmetric monoidal functor
{\mathbf{Fun}}(-,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}):{\mathbf{Cat}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}
along
{\mathbf{Set}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}
. The lax symmetric monoidal structure on the latter
is given by the symmetric monoidal structure with the maximal tensor product
on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
viewed as an object in
{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Cat}})
. We denote the corresponding bifunctor by
\hat{\boxtimes}
. It is given for
X,Y
in
\mathrm{LCH}
and
(A_{x})_{x\in X}
in
\Phi(X)
and
(B_{y})_{y\in Y}
in
\Phi(Y)
by

(A_{x})_{x\in X}\hat{\boxtimes}(B_{y})_{y\in Y}=(A_{x}\otimes B_{y})_{(x,y)\in X\times Y}

in
\Phi(X\times Y)
.
We next construct the natural transformation

N:(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\Phi\ ,\quad X\mapsto\left(N_{X}:XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\Phi(X),\quad A\mapsto(A(x))_{x\in X}\right)\ .

For a morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathrm{LCH}
we have a canonical commutative square

\textstyle{YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\scriptstyle{N_{Y}}
\textstyle{XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{N_{X}}
\textstyle{\Phi(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi(f)}
\textstyle{\Phi(X)}

(5.22)

given on
B
in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
by the obvious identification induced by (
5.12
) at the lower right corner of

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 25.7613pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-7.0434pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 7.04341pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 106.55966pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-23.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 106.55966pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{f^{*}B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 118.8878pt\raise-7.44444pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 118.8878pt\raise-23.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-25.7613pt\raise-31.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(B(y))_{y\in Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.7613pt\raise-31.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.7613pt\raise-31.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.7613pt\raise-31.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(B(f(x))_{x\in X}\cong((f^{*}B)(x))_{x\in X}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

(5.23)

If
g:Y\to Z
is a second map, then the two fillers of the squares corresponding to
f
and
g
compose to the filler of the square corresponding to
g\circ f
. This finishes the construction of the transformation
N
.

For every
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
the functor
N_{X}
is faithful since for every
A
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the homomorphism

{\mathtt{ev}}=({\mathtt{ev}}_{x})_{x}:A\to\prod_{x\in X}A(x)

is injective by
5.16
. So the map

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A,B)\ni f\mapsto(f_{x}:={\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(f):A(x)\to B(x))_{x\in X}\in{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\Phi(X)}(N_{X}(A),N_{X}(B))

is injective.

The transformation
N
thus presents

(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
as a subfunctor of
\Phi
.
We want to construct the lax symmetric monoidal structure on
(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
by restriction along
N
. To this end, we must provide, for every
X,Y
in
\mathrm{LCH}
, the bifunctor

\boxtimes:XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\times YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to(X\times Y)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

(5.24)

such that

N_{X\times Y}(A\boxtimes B)\cong N_{X}(A)\hat{\boxtimes}N_{Y}(B)\ .

(5.25)

We then show that the structure maps can be inherited from those of
\Phi
.

We start with the bifunctor in (
5.24
) whose construction was already proposed in
[
EW98
, Sec. 2]
.
For
A
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
B
in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
we provisionally define the underlying
C^{*}
-algebra of

A\boxtimes B
as the maximal tensor product
A\otimes B
.
The structure of
A\boxtimes B
as an
(X\times Y)
-algebra is given by

C_{0}(X\times Y)\otimes(A\otimes B)\cong(C_{0}(X)\otimes A)\otimes(C_{0}(Y)\otimes B)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle m\otimes n}}{{\to}}A\otimes B\ ,

where
m
and
n
are the multiplication maps for
A
and
B
, and
we use the isomorphism

C_{0}(X\times Y)\cong C_{0}(X)\otimes C_{0}(Y)
, and the fact that the maximal tensor product of two surjections is again surjective. We have a family of maps

{\mathtt{ev}}_{(x,y)}:={\mathtt{ev}}_{x}\otimes{\mathtt{ev}}_{y}:A\otimes B\to A(x)\otimes B(y)\ ,\quad(x,y)\in X\times Y

which provide a map

{\mathtt{ev}}:A\otimes B\to\prod_{(x,y)\in X\times Y}A(x)\otimes B(y)\ .

(5.26)

Lemma 5.41

(
[
EW98
, Sec. 2]
)
.

The map
{\mathtt{ev}}
in (
5.26
) is injective, its image satisfies the equivalent conditions in
5.22
, and
we have an isomorphism

(A\boxtimes B)(x,y)=A(x)\otimes B(y)\ .

Proof.

By the exactness of the maximal tensor product, for every
(x,y)
we have an exact sequence (see
7.10
)

0\to A(X\setminus\{x\})\otimes B+A\otimes B(Y\setminus\{y\})\to A\otimes B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{ev}}_{(x,y)}}}{{\to}}A(x)\otimes B(y)\to 0\ .

The kernel of
{\mathtt{ev}}_{(x,y)}
is generated by
elements of the form

{\mathtt{pr}}_{X}^{*}fa\otimes{\mathtt{pr}}_{Y}^{*}gb+{\mathtt{pr}}_{X}f^{\prime}a\otimes{\mathtt{pr}}_{Y}^{*}g^{\prime}b
, where
f,f^{\prime}
are in
C_{0}(X)
with
f(x)=0
and
g,g^{\prime}
are in
C_{0}(Y)
with
g^{\prime}(y)=0
.
We can write this element as

(m\otimes n)(({\mathtt{pr}}_{X}^{*}f\otimes{\mathtt{pr}}_{Y}^{*}g+{\mathtt{pr}}_{X}f^{\prime}\otimes{\mathtt{pr}}_{Y}^{*}g^{\prime})\otimes a\otimes b)\ .

The functions of the form
({\mathtt{pr}}_{X}^{*}f\otimes{\mathtt{pr}}_{Y}^{*}g+{\mathtt{pr}}_{X}f^{\prime}\otimes{\mathtt{pr}}_{Y}^{*}g^{\prime})
generate

C_{0}(X\times Y\setminus\{(x,y)\})
.
It follows that

{\mathtt{ker}}({\mathtt{ev}}_{(x,y)})=(A\boxtimes B)(X\times Y\setminus\{(x,y)\})
.
We conclude that

(A\boxtimes B)(x,y)=A(x)\otimes B(y)\ .

(5.27)

and that

{\mathtt{ev}}
in (
5.26
) is injective by
5.16
.
∎

In view of the above considerations, we now change the definition of
\boxtimes
by replacing

A\boxtimes B
with its isomorphic image under
{\mathtt{ev}}
in
\prod_{(x,y)}A(x)\otimes B(y)
. Then
A\boxtimes B

is generated by the products of
the constant sections

({\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a)\otimes{\mathtt{ev}}_{y}(b))_{(x,y)}
with elements of
C_{0}(X\times Y)
.
The relation (
5.25
) follows from (
5.27
).

By
5.18
, we have an isomorphism

A\boxtimes B\cong C_{0}(X\times Y,A\otimes B)/I\ ,

(5.28)

where
I
consists of functions
\phi
such that

({\mathtt{ev}}_{x}\otimes{\mathtt{ev}}_{y})(\phi)=0
for all
(x,y)
in
X\times Y
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathrm{LCH}
and
Z
in
\mathrm{LCH}
.
Then we have a commutative square

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 31.89583pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.89583pt\raise (5.29)

On
(A,B)
in
\Phi(Y)\times\Phi(Z)

it is given by the obvious identity

\textstyle{((A_{y})_{y\in Y},(B_{z})_{z\in Z})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(5.30)

at the lower right corner. If

is in

and

and

and

, then
this identity is the image
of an isomorphism

under

of a morphism in

which defines the filler of

3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 134.52344pt\raise-23.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-40.39299pt\raise-31.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(Y\times Z)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.9044pt\raise-25.17513pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.79709pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(f\times{\mathtt{id}})^{*}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 93.60962pt\raise-31.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 93.60962pt\raise-31.72221pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(X\times Z)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

(5.31)

Indeed, the identification preserves constant sections generated by elements
a\otimes b
for
a
in
A
and
b
in
B
and commutes with the multiplication by functions in
C_{0}(X\times Z)
.
This finishes the description of the data of the bifunctor
\boxtimes
.
The necessary relations are inherited from those of
\hat{\boxtimes}
.

We now consider the structure maps of the lax symmetric monoidal structure.
We consider the associators. For
X,Y,Z
in
\mathrm{LCH}
we have the isomorphism

(\Phi(X)\hat{\boxtimes}\Phi(Y))\hat{\boxtimes}\Phi(Z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\Phi(a)(\Phi(X)\hat{\boxtimes}(\Phi(Y)\hat{\boxtimes}\Phi(Z)))

in
\Phi((X\times Y)\times Z)

induced by the associator on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
,
where
a:(X\times Y)\times Z\to X\times(Y\times Z)
is the associator of the structure on
\mathrm{LCH}
.
On objects
A,B,C
in the respective categories, this associator map is given by
the family of associators

(A_{x}\otimes B_{y})\otimes C_{z}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}A_{x}\otimes(B_{y}\otimes C_{z})

(5.32)

for all
((x,y),z)
in
(X\times Y)\times Z
.
We must show that this map is the image under
N_{(X\times Y)\times Z}
of an isomorphism

(A\boxtimes B)\boxtimes C\to a^{*}(A\boxtimes(B\boxtimes C))\ .

(5.33)

The family of maps (
5.32
) induces a map

\prod_{((x,y),z)\in(X\times Y)\times Z}(A(x)\otimes B(y))\otimes C(z)\to\prod_{((x,y),z)\in(X\times Y)\times Z}A(x)\otimes(B(y)\otimes C(z))\ .

This map is compatible with the multiplication by
C_{0}((X\times Y)\times Z)
and preserves constant sections.
Consequently, it restricts to a map as in (
5.33
), which under

N_{(X\times Y)\times Z}
is mapped to the family (
5.32
).

We obtain the unit and symmetry constraints for
\boxtimes
in a similar manner. The necessary relations are inherited from those of
\hat{\boxtimes}
.
∎

Corollary 5.42
.

The functor
(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}

refines to a functor with values in
{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Cat}})
.

Proof.

This is the standard fact using that the symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}
is induced by the cartesian symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathrm{LCH}
. More concretely, the reason is that every object of
\mathrm{LCH}
is a coalgebra with the diagonal as structure map.
∎

We let
\otimes_{X}
denote the induced symmetric monoidal structure on
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. In the literature, it is called the maximal
balanced tensor product over
X

[
Bla95
]
,
[
EW98
]
,
[
PT00
]
,
[
Pop04
]
.

Remark 5.43
.

For
A,B
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, we have

A\otimes_{X}B\cong d^{*}(A\boxtimes B)\ ,

where
d:X\to X\times X
is the diagonal.
For
f:X\to Y
, the commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{d_{X}}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{Y}}
\textstyle{X\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f\times f}
\textstyle{Y\times Y}

(5.34)

again implies that

f^{*}:YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

canonically refines to a symmetric monoidal functor.

∎

For the proof of
Theorem

5.2
, we must verify various
projection formulas.
Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathrm{LCH}
.

Lemma 5.44
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a morphism in
\mathrm{LCH}
,
A
be in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
B
be in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. Then
we have an isomorphism

f_{!}(A)\otimes_{Y}B\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}f_{!}(A\otimes_{X}f^{*}B)\ .

(5.35)

Proof.

By definition

f_{!}(A)\otimes_{Y}B\subseteq\prod_{y\in Y}\left(\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}A(x)\right)\otimes B(y)

is generated by sections of the form

((\kappa(x){\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a))_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}\otimes{\mathtt{ev}}_{y}(b))_{y\in Y}

(5.36)

for
a
in
A
,
b
in
B
and
\kappa
in
C_{0}(X)
.
Similarly

f_{!}(A\otimes_{X}f^{*}B)\subseteq\prod_{y\in Y}\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}\left(A(x)\otimes B(f(x))\right)

is generated by sections of the form

(({\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a)\otimes\kappa(x){\mathtt{ev}}_{f(x)}(b))_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})})_{y\in Y}

(5.37)

for
a
in
A
,
b
in
B
, and
\kappa
in
C_{0}(X)
. The map
(
5.35
) will be obtained by restriction of
the canonical map

\prod_{y\in Y}\left(\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}A(x)\right)\otimes B(y)\to\prod_{y\in Y}\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}\left(A(x)\otimes B(f(x))\right)\ .

(5.38)

This map sends a generator of the form (
5.36
)
to the generator

(({\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a)\otimes\kappa(x){\mathtt{ev}}_{f(x)}(b))_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})})_{y\in Y}\ ,

which has the form (
5.37
).
Consequently, the map (
5.38
) restricts to a map (
5.35
). Vice versa,
the generator (
5.37
) has a preimage of the form (
5.36
).
This already shows that (
5.35
) is surjective. We now show that this map is injective.
We consider an element
c
in the kernel. It suffices to check injectivity on the level of fibres at the points
y
in
Y
.
We use the notation
i:\{y\}\to Y
,
j:f^{-1}(\{y\})\to X
and
k:f^{-1}(\{y\})\to\{y\}
.
The map is then given by

\displaystyle(f_{!}A\otimes_{Y}B)(y)\cong i^{*}(f_{!}A\otimes_{Y}B)\cong(f_{!}A)(y)\otimes B(y)\cong(j^{*}A)(f^{-1}(\{y\}))\otimes B(y)

\displaystyle\to(j^{*}A\otimes_{f^{-1}(\{y\})}k^{*}B(y))(f^{-1}(\{y\}))\cong j^{*}(A\otimes_{X}f^{*}B)(f^{-1}(\{y\}))\cong f_{!}(A\otimes_{X}f^{*}B)(y)

It suffices to show that

(j^{*}A)(f^{-1}(\{y\}))\otimes B(y)\to(j^{*}A\otimes_{f^{-1}(\{y\})}k^{*}B(y))(f^{-1}(\{y\}))

is injective.
But this map has an inverse which sends
a generator
({\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a)\otimes\kappa(x){\mathtt{ev}}_{y}(b))_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}

to
(\kappa(x){\mathtt{ev}}_{x}(a)\otimes{\mathtt{ev}}_{y}(b))_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}
.
∎

Remark 5.45
.

Note that the projection formula in
5.44
does not require any condition on the map
f
. But for the proof of
Theorem

5.2
, we must know that
for
f
a proper map or an open inclusion, this isomorphism is given by the canonical map coming from the lax symmetric monoidal structure and the units or counits of the respective adjunctions. This is the content of
5.46

and
5.48
below.

∎

Lemma 5.46
.

If
f:X\to Y
is an open inclusion,
A
is in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
B
is in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
,
then the canonical morphism

f_{!}(A\otimes f^{*}B)\to f_{!}(A)\otimes B

(5.39)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Note that the canonical morphism (
5.39
) is

f_{!}(A\otimes f^{*}B)\to f_{!}(f^{*}f_{!}A\otimes f^{*}B)\cong f_{!}f^{*}(f_{!}A\otimes B)\to f_{!}A\otimes B\ ,

which uses the fact that
f^{*}
is symmetric monoidal and the unit and counit of the adjunction
f_{!}\dashv f^{*}
.
Using the explicit formulas for the operations and for the unit and counit from
5.34
,
the canonical morphism (
5.39
) is the restriction of the map

\displaystyle\prod_{y\in Y}\left(\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}A(x)\otimes B(f(x))\right)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\prod_{y\in Y}\left(\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}\left(\prod_{x^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(\{f(x)\})}A(x^{\prime})\right)\otimes B(f(x))\right)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\prod_{y\in Y}\left(\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}A(x)\otimes B(f(x))\right)

where the first map is the diagonal and the second map is the evaluation at
x=x^{\prime}
.
Using the fact that
f
is injective, these maps are both isomorphisms.
∎

Remark 5.47
.

By an inspection one observes that the canonical map (
5.39
) is equal to the inverse of the isomorphism (
5.35
).

Lemma 5.48
.

If
f:X\to Y
is a proper morphism in
\mathrm{LCH}
,
A
is in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
B
is in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, then
the canonical morphism

f_{!}(A)\otimes B\to f_{!}(A\otimes f^{*}B)

(5.40)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Note that the canonical morphism (
5.40
) is given by

f_{!}(A)\otimes B\to f_{!}f^{*}(f_{!}A\otimes B)\cong f_{!}(f^{*}f_{!}A\otimes f^{*}B)\to f_{!}(A\otimes f^{*}B)

(5.41)

which uses the fact that
f^{*}
is symmetric monoidal and the unit and counit of the adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{!}
.
We use the explicit formulas for the operations and units and counits in order to write (
5.40
) as the restriction of the composition

\displaystyle\prod_{y\in Y}\left(\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}A(x)\right)\otimes B(y)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\prod_{y\in Y}\prod_{x^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}\left(\left(\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{f(x^{\prime})\})}A(x)\right)\otimes B(y)\right)

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\prod_{y\in Y}\prod_{x^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}\left(\left(\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{f(x^{\prime})\})}A(x)\right)\otimes B(f(x^{\prime}))\right)

\displaystyle\to

\displaystyle\prod_{y\in Y}\left(\prod_{x\in f^{-1}(\{y\})}A(x)\otimes B(y)\right)

where the first map is the diagonal and the second is the evaluation at
x=x^{\prime}
.
This is precisely the map (
5.38
).
Hence, the assertion follows from
5.44
.
∎

5.7
Proof of
Theorem

5.2

By
5.40
and
5.42
we have a functor

(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Cat}})\ .

(5.42)

We must verify the conditions from
6.7
.

We argue that the functor in (
5.42
)
is compatible with
I
. The existence of left-adjoints for open embeddings
follows from
5.34
. The base change
and projection formulas have been verified in
5.37

and
5.46
.

The functor in (
5.42
) is furthermore compatible with
P
. The existence
of right-adjoints for proper maps follows from
5.32
. The base change
and projection formulas have been verified in
5.38

and
5.48
.

Finally the mixed Beck-Chevalley condition follows from the fact that
for proper maps
f
, the right-adjoint of
f^{*}
is given by the the covariant functoriality
f_{!}
.

5.8
Good objects

In this section, we analyze to what extend the functor
c
in (
5.9
) preserves exact sequences. Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space, and let
A
be in
\mathrm{Open}(X)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Then we can consider
C_{0}(X)\otimes A\cong C_{0}(X,A)
.
In this algebra, we have two ideals:

1.

The ideal
J
is generated by tensors
\phi\otimes a,
where

\phi\in C_{0}(U)
and
a\in A(V)
for some open sets
U,V
in
X
with
U\cap V=\emptyset
.

2.

The ideal
I
(used in the proof of
Theorem

5.15
) which consists of functions
\phi
in
C_{0}(X,A)
with the property
that
\phi(x)\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
for all
x
in
X
.

Lemma 5.49
.

We have an inclusion
J\subseteq I
.

Proof.

Assume that
\phi\otimes a
is a generator of
J
as in
1
.
Then
\phi\otimes a
corresponds to the function
x\mapsto\phi(x)a
.
If
x\not\in U
, then
\phi(x)a=0\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
since
\phi(x)=0
. If
x\in U
, then
x\not\in V
and hence
V\subseteq X\setminus\{x\}
.
Consequently,
\phi(x)a\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
since
a\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
.
This implies that all generators of
J
belong to
I
and therefore proves the assertion.
∎

Definition 5.50
.

An object of
A
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is called good if
I=J
.
We let
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{gd}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Open}(X)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
on
objects that are good.

In contrast to the conventions used elsewhere in the present paper, a sequence in
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{gd}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
will be called exact if it is exact in
\mathrm{Open}(X)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Our main reason for introducing good objects is the following fact:

Lemma 5.51
.

The restriction

c_{|\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{gd}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}:\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{gd}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

of the functor
c
in (
5.8
)
preserves exact sequences.

Proof.

Assume that

0\to A\to B\to C\to 0

is exact in
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{gd}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. Then we must show that

0\to c(A)\to c(B)\to c(C)\to 0

is exact in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. We use that by
5.12
, exactness in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
can be checked on the underlying algebras.
Let
I,I^{\prime},I^{\prime\prime}
and
J,J^{\prime},J^{\prime\prime}
be the ideals associated to
A,B
, and
C
respectively.
We consider the map of vertical exact sequences

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 215.56302pt\raise-120.0178pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 51.459pt\raise-126.96225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 130.6704pt\raise-126.96225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 210.06302pt\raise-126.96225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 269.70325pt\raise-126.96225pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

By homological algebra and the exactness of the middle sequence, we get isomorphisms

\mathrm{coker}(c(B)\to c(C))\cong 0\ ,

{\mathtt{ker}}(c(A)\to c(B))\cong{\mathtt{ker}}(I^{\prime}\to I^{\prime\prime})/{\mathtt{im}}(I\to I^{\prime})\ ,

and

{\mathtt{ker}}(c(B)\to c(C))/{\mathtt{im}}(c(A)\to c(B))\cong\mathrm{coker}(I^{\prime}\to I^{\prime\prime})\ .

Assume now that
\phi
is in
{\mathtt{ker}}(I^{\prime}\to I^{\prime\prime})
.
Then
\phi\in C_{0}(X,A)
and
\phi(x)\in B(X\setminus\{x\})
for all
x
in
X
.
Hence
\phi(x)\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
for all
x
and hence
\phi\in I
.
It follows that
{\mathtt{ker}}(c(A)\to c(B))\cong 0
.

In order to show that
{\mathtt{ker}}(c(B)\to c(C))/{\mathtt{im}}(c(A)\to c(B))\cong 0
we use
goodness. It implies that
I^{\prime\prime}
is generated by
elements of the form
\phi\otimes c
with
\phi\in C_{0}(U)
and
c\in C(X\setminus\overline{U})
for
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
.
Since
B(X\setminus\overline{U})\to C(X\setminus\overline{U})
is surjective, we can lift such elements to generators of
I^{\prime}
.
Hence
I^{\prime}\to I^{\prime\prime}
is surjective, which implies the desired result.
∎

Lemma 5.52
.

We have
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\subseteq\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{gd}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

Proof.

We consider
A
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
. Let

\phi
be in
I
. We must show that
\phi\in J
. It suffices to show that

\phi
can be approximated by elements of
J
.

We first show that elements
\phi
in
I
can be approximated by elements with compact support. Since the condition
\phi\in I
is pointwise, for all
\kappa
in
C_{0}(X)
we also have
\kappa\phi\in I
.
Fix
\epsilon
in
(0,\infty)
.
The subset
\{x\in X\mid\|\phi(x)\|\geq\epsilon\}
is compact.
We can choose
\kappa
in
C_{c}(X)
such that
\|\kappa\|\leq 1
and
\kappa\equiv 1
on this subset. Then
\|\phi-\kappa\phi\|\leq\epsilon
. Note that
\kappa\phi
has compact support.

From now one, we assume that
\phi
has compact support and show that it can be approximated by elements of
J
.

Fix again
\epsilon
in
(0,\infty)
.
For every
x
in
X

we have
\phi(x)\in A(X\setminus\{x\})
. By regularity of
A

we have

A(X\setminus\{x\})=\overline{\bigcup_{U\Subset X\setminus\{x\}}A(U)}\ .

Using this and continuity of
\phi
we can therefore
fix an open neighborhood
U_{x}
of
x
and
a_{x}
in
A(X\setminus\bar{U}_{x})

such that

\|\phi(x)-a_{x}\|\leq\epsilon/2\ ,\quad\|\phi(x)-\phi(y)\|\leq\epsilon/2

for all
y
in
U_{x}
.
In total, we have

\|a_{x}-\phi(y)\|\leq\epsilon

for all
y
in
U_{x}
.

Since
{\mathtt{supp}}(\phi)
is compact, we can choose a finite
set
(x_{k})_{k\in K}
of points in
X
such that
{\mathtt{supp}}(\phi)\subseteq\bigcup_{k\in K}U_{x_{k}}
.
We further choose a partition of unity
(\chi_{k})_{k\in K}
subordinated to this covering.
Then for every
y
in
X
we have

\phi(y)=\sum_{k\in K}\chi_{k}(y)\phi(y)\ .

We define

\tilde{\phi}(y):=\sum_{k\in K}\chi_{k}(y)\otimes a_{x_{k}}\ .

Then,

\|\phi(y)-\tilde{\phi}(y)\|\leq\sum_{k\in K}\chi_{k}(y)\|\phi(y)-a_{x_{k}})\|\leq\sum_{k\in K}\chi_{k}(y)\epsilon=\epsilon\ .

By construction, we have
\tilde{\phi}\in J
and
\|\phi-\tilde{\phi}\|\leq\epsilon
.

We conclude that every element of
I
can be approximated by elements of
J
. ∎

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space, let
Y
be a locale, and let
f:X\to Y
be a map of locales.
We let
f^{-1}:\mathcal{P}(Y)\to\mathrm{Open}(X)
be the corresponding map of frames. The following result provides an extension of the adjunction obtained in
5.32
to a bigger class of maps.
In the following statement,
f_{!}
is the morphism of
E
-theory contexts as in
3.26
.

Lemma 5.53
.

If
f^{-1}
is perfect (
4.4
), then we have an adjunction

f^{*}:YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\leftrightarrows XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:f_{!}

(5.43)

and
f^{*}
is a morphism of
E
-theory contexts.

Proof.

By
4.3
, we have the adjunction

f^{-1}:\mathcal{P}(Y)\leftrightarrows\mathrm{Open}(X):f_{\sharp}

(5.44)

in
\mathbf{Poset}^{\mathrm{le}}
, where
\mathcal{P}(Y)
denotes the poset corresponding to
Y
. Since
(-)^{\mathrm{le}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is a functor to
E
-theory contexts defined on
\mathbf{Poset}^{\mathrm{le}}
we get an adjunction

f^{\sharp}:\mathcal{P}(Y)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\leftrightarrows\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{le}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:f_{!}\ ,

(5.45)

where
f_{!}=(f^{-1})^{*}
and
f^{\sharp}=(f_{\sharp})^{*}
are morphisms of
E
-theory contexts.
If
f^{-1}
is perfect, then

f_{\sharp}
preserves filtered joins,
and the adjunction (
5.44
) lifts to one in
\mathbf{Poset}^{\mathrm{prfr}}_{(*)}
(see
4.5
). Since
(-)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is a functor to
E
-theory contexts defined on
\mathbf{Poset}_{(*)}^{\mathrm{prfr}}
we then get
an adjunction

f^{\sharp}:\mathcal{P}(Y)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\leftrightarrows\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:f_{!}\ ,

(5.46)

where again
f_{!}
and
f^{*}
are morphisms of
E
-theory contexts.
In particular,
f^{\sharp}
takes values in
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{gd}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
by
5.52
.
Composing the adjunctions (
5.46
) and (
5.8
),
we finally get the adjunction

f^{*}:YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\leftrightarrows XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:f_{!}

(5.47)

with
f^{*}=c\circ f^{\sharp}
and
c
as in (
5.8
).
Since
c
preserves filtered colimits, finite products, and also exact sequences when restricted to

\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{gd}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
we see that
f^{*}
is also a morphism of
E
-theory contexts.
∎

5.9
Proof of
Theorem

5.1

By
5.40
, we have the lax symmetric monoidal functor

(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}\ ,\quad X\mapsto XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,\quad f\mapsto f^{*}\ .

We must show that it satisfies the conditions listed in
2.14
.

We clearly have
\{*\}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\simeq C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
as a symmetric monoidal category.

Recall from
2.2
the definition of an
E
-admissible map.

Lemma 5.54
.

For every morphism
f:X\to Y
in
\mathrm{LCH}

the functor

f^{*}:XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
is
E
-admissible.

Proof.

We must show that
f^{*}
preserves finite products, filtered colimits, and exact sequences.

We first assume that
f
is proper. Then
f^{*}
is a left-adjoint and preserves colimits.
In order to see that it preserves exact sequences and finite products,
we use the bold commutative diagram and the adjoints as indicated.
