3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 120.32089pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

Since
\mathrm{incl}
is fully faithful, we get
f^{*}\simeq c\circ f^{\sharp}\circ\mathrm{incl}
, where
f^{\sharp}
is the pull-back along the right-adjoint in the poset adjunction

f^{-1}:\mathrm{Open}(Y)\leftrightarrows\mathrm{Open}(X):f_{\sharp}

which is a preframe morphism since
f
is proper, and therefore
f^{-1}
is a perfect frame morphism (see
4.5
and
4.7
).
Now
c
,
f^{\sharp}
and
\mathrm{incl}
are all
E
-admissible. In detail,
in order to see that
c
preserves exact sequences, we use that regular objects are good and that
c
restricted to good objects preserves
exact sequences, see
5.51
. Further
c
preserves finite products by an inspection of the constructions.
Since
c
is a left-adjoint, it preserves colimits.

By a combination of

3.19
and
3.23
, the functor
\mathrm{incl}

is
E
-admissible.
The morphism
f^{\sharp}
is the pull-back morphism along a preframe morphism,
and is therefore
E
-admissible by
3.15
.
We conclude that
f^{*}
is
E
-admissible provided
f
is proper.

We now assume that
f:X\to Y
is an open embedding. By
5.34
,
the functor
f^{*}
is the right-adjoint of the functor
f_{!}
. By
4.10
,
the right-adjoint of
f_{!}
is
E
-admissible. Therefore

f^{*}
is
E
-admissible.

Since every morphism
f
in
\mathrm{LCH}
is a composition of a proper map and an open inclusion, we conclude that
f^{*}
is
E
-admissible for every morphism
f
in
\mathrm{LCH}
. ∎

Lemma 5.55
.

For
X
and
Y
in
\mathrm{LCH}
and
every
A
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the functor

A\boxtimes-:YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to(X\times Y)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

is
E
-admissible.

Proof.

We start by showing that it preserves exact sequences.
Let

0\to B\to C\to D\to 0

be an exact sequence in
YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
We must show that then

0\to A\boxtimes B\to A\boxtimes C\to A\boxtimes D\to 0

is exact in
(X\times Y)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
By
5.12
.
2
and
5.12
.
3
, the exactness of this sequence can be checked on the level of underlying algebras. In view of the construction of
\boxtimes
given in the proof of
5.40
,
the assertion follows from the fact that

0\to A\otimes B\to A\otimes C\to A\otimes D\to 0

is exact in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
since the maximal tensor product preserves exact sequences.

Using (
5.28
) it is easy to see that
A\boxtimes-
preserves finite products.

In order to show that that
A\boxtimes-
preserves filtered colimits,
we again use
5.12
.
1
and that the maximal tensor product
A\otimes-

on
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
preserves filtered colimits.
∎

5.54
and
5.55
verify the remaining conditions of
2.14
.

The following consequence of
Theorem

5.1
is surely well-known, but
a proof is difficult to locate in the literature.

Corollary 5.56
.

For every locally compact Hausdorff space
X
and
A
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

the (maximal balanced) tensor product

A\otimes_{X}-:XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

preserves exact sequences and filtered colimits.

Proof.

Using the diagonal
d:X\to X\times X
we have an isomorphism
of functors

A\otimes_{X}-\cong d^{*}\circ(A\boxtimes-)\ .

Since the right-hand side
is a composition of two
E
-admissible functors, it is itself
E
-admissible. So
A\otimes_{X}-
preserves in particular
filtered colimits and exact sequences.
∎

5.10
Approximation by finite spaces

For a locally compact Hausdorff space
the collection of evaluations

(XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ni A\mapsto A(U)\in C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})_{U\in\mathrm{Open}(X)}

is clearly conservative. The fact that this remains to be true on the level of
E
-theory
is a crucial input for the proof of our main
Theorem

1.9
.
In view of our construction of
E
-theory by forcing the universal properties,
we do not have a direct access to the mapping spaces. The only way to get some control
is to use adjunctions like in
5.58
below in order to relate mapping spaces in the
E
-theory
of complicated spaces with those in the
E
-theory of simpler spaces.
The proof of the following
theorem, which states in a condensed way that the collection of evaluations

(E(X)\ni A\mapsto A(U)\in\mathrm{E})_{U\in\mathrm{Open}(X)}

is jointly conservative,
applies this idea.
Recall the morphism
s_{X}:E(X)\to\mathrm{CoShv}(X,\mathrm{E})
from
4.17
.

Theorem 5.57
.

For every
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
the functor
s_{X}:E(X)\to\mathrm{CoShv}(X,\mathrm{E})
is conservative.

Proof.

Let
X
be a locally compact Hausdorff space, and let
f:X\to Y
be a map to a finite locale. Since
Y
is finite, the map
f^{-1}:\mathcal{P}(Y)\to\mathrm{Open}(X)
is perfect. Consequently, by

5.53

the adjunction

f^{*}:YC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\leftrightarrows XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:f_{!}\ .

(5.48)

is an adjunction of
E
-theory contexts.

Corollary 5.58
.

If
X
is a locally compact Hausdorff space, and
f:X\to Y
is a morphism to a finite locale, then
we have an adjunction

f^{*}:E(Y)\leftrightarrows E(X):f_{!}\ .

(5.49)

Lemma 5.59
.

If
X
is a topological space, then
there exists a filtered system
(Y_{I})_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}}
of finite sober topological spaces such that

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}}\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})\cong\mathrm{Open}(X)

in
\mathbf{Poset}
.

Proof.

We let

{\mathbf{I}}
be the filtered system of finite subframes
(\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I}))_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}}
of
\mathrm{Open}(X)
.
The set of points of
\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})
will be denoted by
Y_{I}
. These points are the frame morphisms
y:\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})\to\{0\leq 1\}
. For every
x
in
X
we get a point

f_{I}(x):U\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}0&x\not\in U\\
1&x\in U\end{array}\right.\ .

We claim that
\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})
is spatial. In fact, if
U,V
are in
\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})
and
U\not\subseteq V
, then there exists
x
in
V\setminus U
. Then
f_{I}(x)(V)=1
and
f_{I}(x)(U)=0
.
It follows that

\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})
is precisely the topology on the topological space
Y_{I}
and

f_{I}:X\to Y_{i}
is a continuous map.

An inclusion
I\subseteq I^{\prime}
in
{\mathbf{I}}
is an inclusion of frames, and hence induces a surjective map of topological spaces
f_{I^{\prime},I}:Y_{I^{\prime}}\to Y_{I}
. They are the structure maps of the functor

{\mathbf{I}}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Locale}\ ,\quad I\mapsto Y_{I}\ .

For every
I
in
{\mathbf{I}}
we have a surjective map of locales

f_{I}:X\to Y_{I}
.
We claim that these maps present
\mathrm{Open}(X)
as the colimit

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}}\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\cong}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Open}(X)

(5.50)

in
\mathbf{Poset}
.
Indeed, since every open subset of
U
of
X
belongs to
\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})
for some
I
in
{\mathbf{I}}
this is an isomorphism of underlying sets which turns out to be a poset-isomorphism.
∎

Remark 5.60
.

One must be careful, as (
5.50
) is not true when one interprets the colimit in frames.
The colimit in frames is bigger.
On other words, we have a map of locales

X\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}^{\mathrm{op}}}Y_{I}

which is not an isomorphism in general.
∎

We apply
5.59
to a locally compact Hausdorff space
X
.

Lemma 5.61
.

If
X
is a locally compact Hausdorff space, then the canonical functor

E(X)\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}^{\mathrm{op}}}E(Y_{I})

is fully faithful.

Proof.

In view of the presence of the adjunctions
f_{I}^{*}\dashv f_{I,!}
obtained in
5.58
,
we must show that for every
A
in
E(X)
the natural transformation

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}}f_{I}^{*}f_{I,!}A\to A

(5.51)

is an equivalence. These adjunctions come from adjunctions (
5.48
) on the level of
E
-theory contexts.
We first show that for every
A^{\prime}
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
the transformation

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}}f_{I}^{*}f_{I,!}A^{\prime}\to A^{\prime}

is an isomorphism. Since
c
commutes with colimits
and
c\circ\mathrm{incl}\simeq{\mathtt{id}}
for
\mathrm{incl}:XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}

this is equivalent to the assertion that

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}}f_{I}^{\sharp}f_{I,!}A^{\prime}\to A^{\prime}

is an isomorphism in
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
, where
f^{\sharp}_{I}
is as in (
5.46
) and we omitted the functor
\mathrm{incl}
for better readability.
Using the adjunctions (
5.46
),
we must show for every
B^{\prime}
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
that

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\cong\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}}{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(f_{I,!}A^{\prime},f_{I,!}B^{\prime})\ .

The right-hand side is the intersection over
I
in
{\mathbf{I}}
of the subsets of
{\mathtt{Hom}}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A,B)
of homomorphisms
which are compatible with the
\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
-structure. This intersection is precisely

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathrm{Open}(X)^{\mathrm{reg}}C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
since every open subset
U
of
X
belongs to
\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})
for some
I
in
{\mathbf{I}}
.

Since
\mathrm{e}:XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to E(X)
preserves filtered colimits, we see that
the map (
5.51
), is an equivalence for
A=\mathrm{e}(A^{\prime})
for every
A^{\prime}
in
XC^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
By the universal property of
\mathrm{e}
it is an eqivalence for all
A
in
E(X)
.
∎

Corollary 5.62
.

The collection of functors

(f_{I,!}:E(X)\to E(Y_{I}))_{I\in{\mathbf{I}}}

is jointly conservative.

We now invoke
Theorem

4.18
which states the equivalences

E(Y_{I})\simeq\mathrm{CoShv}(Y_{I},\mathrm{E})

for all
I
in
{\mathbf{I}}
.
Since equivalences on cosheaves are detected objectwise, we conclude that
the collection

\left((F\mapsto F(U)):E(Y_{I})\to\mathrm{E}\right)_{U\in\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})}

is jointly conservative.
By unfolding definitions, we see that for
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(Y_{I})
we have the equivalence

s_{X}(A)(U)\simeq s_{Y_{I}}(f_{I,!}A)(U)\ .

Combining this with
5.62
we conclude that
the functor
s_{X}:E(X)\to\mathrm{CoShv}(X,\mathrm{E})
is conservative. This finishes the proof of
Theorem

5.57

∎

Remark 5.63
.

The classical analog of
Theorem

5.57
is
[
DM12
, Thm. 3.10]
.
∎

6
Functor formlisms

6.1
Three functor formalisms

Following
[
HM24
]
,
[
Sch25
]
,
[
Zhu25
]
, we consider the Nagata set-up
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
on the category
\mathrm{LCH}
of locally compact Hausdorff spaces, with the subclasses of morphisms

•

I
: the open inclusions

•

P
: the proper maps.

We consider a functor

D:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty})

(6.1)

Remark 6.1
.

Since the symmetric monoidal structure on
\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}
is induced by the cartesian structure on
\mathrm{LCH}
this datum is equivalent to the datum of a lax symmetric monoidal functor

D:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty}\ .

∎

Remark 6.2
.

We will usually denote the contravariant functoriality of
D
on morphisms
f
in
\mathrm{LCH}
by
f^{*}
,
but sometimes we also use the notation
D(f)
, in particular when there are different functors floating around. In the case of sheaves, we will decorate the operations with
\hat{}
, i.e., we write
\hat{f}^{*}
.
∎

For
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
we will denote the tensor unit and tensor product in
D(X)
by
1_{X}
and
\otimes_{X}
.

We will consider the following groups of conditions on
D
. They are standard conditions
considered in the theory of six-functor formalisms, as stated, e.g.,
[
HM24
]
,
[
Sch25
]
,
[
Zhu25
]
.
The wording is copied from
[
BV
]
.

Definition 6.3
.

We say that
D
as in (
6.1
) is compatible with
I
if:

1.

(I-la: left adjoints) For every
i:U\to X
in
I
the functor
i^{*}
admits a left adjoint
i_{!}:D(U)\to D(X)
.

2.

(I-bc: base change) For every cartesian square

\textstyle{U\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{Y}

in
\mathrm{LCH}
with
i,j
in
I

the square

\textstyle{D(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j^{*}}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{D(V)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}
\textstyle{D(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}}
\textstyle{D(U)}

is horizontally left-adjoinable.

3.

(I-pf: projection formula) For every
i:U\to X
in
I
the canonical map

i_{!}(-\otimes_{U}i^{*}(-))\to i_{!}(-)\otimes_{X}i^{*}(-)

is an equivalence.

Definition 6.4
.

We say that
D
as in (
6.1
) is compatible with
P
if:

1.

(P-ra: right adjoint) For every
p:X\to Y
in
P
the functor
p^{*}
has a right-adjoint
p_{*}
.

2.

(P-bc: base change) For every cartesian square

\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{Y}

in
\mathrm{LCH}
with
p,q
in
P

the square

\textstyle{D(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\textstyle{D(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{*}}
\textstyle{D(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f^{*}}
\textstyle{D(W)}

is vertically right-adjoinable.

3.

(P-pf: projection formula) For every
p:X\to Y
in
P
the canonical map

p_{*}(-)\otimes_{Y}(-)\to p_{*}(-\otimes_{X}p^{*}(-))

is an equivalence.

If
D
is compatible with
I
and
P
, then we make the following definition:

Definition 6.5
.

We say that
D
satisfies the mixed Beck-Chevalley (mBC) condition if
for every cartesian square

\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{Y}

in
\mathrm{LCH}
with
p,q
in
P

and
i,j
in
I

the square

\textstyle{D(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j_{!}}
\scriptstyle{p^{*}}
\textstyle{D(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{*}}
\textstyle{D(W)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{!}}
\textstyle{D(X)}

given by I-bc
is vertically right-adjoinable.

Let

{\mathcal{B}}:D\to D^{\prime}:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty})

(6.2)

be a natural transformation between functors.
In the following it is useful to write
D(i)
instead of
i^{*}
.

Definition 6.6
.

1.

(I(
{\mathcal{B}}
))
We say that
{\mathcal{B}}
is compatible with
I
if for every morphism
j:U\to X
in
I

the square

\textstyle{D(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{B}}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{D(j)}
\textstyle{D^{\prime}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D^{\prime}(j)}
\textstyle{D(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{B}}_{U}}
\textstyle{D^{\prime}(U)}

is vertically left-adjoinable.

2.

(P(
{\mathcal{B}}
)) We say that
{\mathcal{B}}
is compatible with
P
if for every morphism
p:X\to Y
in
P

the square

\textstyle{D(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{B}}_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{D(p)}
\textstyle{D^{\prime}(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{D^{\prime}(p)}
\textstyle{D(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{{\mathcal{B}}_{X}}
\textstyle{D^{\prime}(X)}

is vertically right-adjoinable.

Definition 6.7
.

1.

A functor

D:\mathrm{LCH}\to{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty})
is called
a three-functor formalism on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
if it is compatible with
I
,
P
, and satisfies the mBC condition.

2.

A morphism between three-functor formalisms on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
is a natural transformation
as in (
6.2
)
which is compatible with
I
and
P
.

Remark 6.8
.

By
[
HM24
, Prop. 3.3.3]
, the datum of a three-functor formalism on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)

in the sense of
6.7
is equivalent to the datum
of a three-functor formalism in the sense of
[
HM24
, Def. 3.1.1]

on the pair
(\mathrm{LCH},\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll})
defined as a lax symmetric monoidal functor

D:\mathrm{Corr}(\mathrm{LCH},\mathcal{A}\mathrm{ll})\to{\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty}\ .

The three functors are
f^{*}
,
f_{!}
, and
\otimes_{X}
.
∎

6.2
Six-functor formalisms

In view of
[
HM24
, Lem. 3.2.5]
, we adopt the following definition.

Definition 6.9
.

1.

A three-functor formalism
D
on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
is a presentable six-functor formalism on
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
if it
factorizes over a functor

D:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\mathbf{Pr}^{L})

and
the right-adjoints
p_{*}
in P-ra (
6.4
.
1
) are cocontinuous.

2.

A morphism between three-functor formalisms on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
is morphism between presentable six-functor formalisms if
it is implemented by left-adjoint functors.

Note that the factorization condition means that
D
takes values in presentable
\infty
-categories and left adoint functors, and that for every
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
the functor
\otimes_{X}
preserves colimits in both arguments.

Remark 6.10
.

We will not need the more general definition of a
{\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty}
-valued six-functor formalism from
[
HM24
, Def. 3.1.2]
which involves more conditions to be checked. All examples of six functor formalisms considered in the present paper are presentable.

∎

Recall
Theorem

5.1
stating that we have a lax symmetric monoidal functor to
E
-theory contexts

(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}\ .

We can now apply
Theorem

2.18
in in order to make the following definition.

Definition 6.11
.

We define the lax symmetric monoidal functor

E(-):=\mathrm{E}((-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}):\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}\to{\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty}\ .

(6.3)

Theorem 6.12
.

The functor in (
6.3
) is a presentable six-functor formalism.

Proof.

By
Theorem

5.2
, the functor in
(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
in (
5.1
) is a three-functor formalism.
We now observe that it takes values in
E
-theory contexts, and that the left-adjoints
i_{!}
for
i
in
I
and the right-adjoints
p_{*}
for
p
in
P
are morphisms of
E
-theory contexts. We conclude that

E(-)
is a three-functor formalism. Since
E(-)
takes values in
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
, and the right-adjoints
\mathrm{E}(p_{*})
for
p
in
P
and the functors

A\otimes_{X}-:E(X)\to E(X)
for all
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
and
A
in
E(X)
are cocontinuous, we conclude that
E
in (
6.3
) is a presentable six-functor formalism.
∎

6.3
Comparison with sheaves

In this section, we compare the presentable six-functor formalism
E
from
Theorem

6.12

with the six-functor formalism
\mathrm{Shv}(-,\mathrm{E})
. We first show that
E
is also a coefficient system.
Since
\mathrm{Shv}(-,\mathrm{E})
plays the role of an initial coefficient system with value
\mathrm{E}
at the point
(this has been shown in a precise sense in
[
Zhu25
]
under slightly stronger assumptions on coefficient systems), we get the natural comparison transformation
6.19
.
The main result of
[
BV
]
, stated here as
Theorem

6.28
, provides a sufficient condition implying that the comparison transformation is an equivalence. We then show
our main
Theorem

6.28
by verifying these conditions for
E
.

Definition 6.13
.

A functor as in (
6.1
) is presentable and stable if it takes values in
{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}})
.

We consider a presentable and stable functor
D
as in (
6.1
), which is compatible with
I
.
The following definition is taken from
[
Zhu25
, Def. 4.11]
.

Definition 6.14
.

We say that
D
has canonical descent if it satisfies:

1.

(zero)
D(\emptyset)\simeq 0
.

2.

(localization) For every
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
, open subset
j:U\to X
and closed complement
i:Z:=X\setminus U\to X
we have a recollement

3.

(open exhaustions)
For every filtered family
(U_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
with
U=\bigcup_{i\in I}U_{i}
we have an equivalence

D(U)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in I^{\mathrm{op}}}D(U_{i})

(6.4)

in
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
induced by contravariant functoriality for the inclusions.

∎

Remark 6.15
.

Compatibility of
D
with
I
first of all ensures by I-la that
j_{!}
exists.
The Condition
1
together with
U\cap Z=\emptyset
and I-bc ensures that
i^{*}\circ j_{!}\simeq 0
. The Condition
2
then requires that
the sequence

D(U)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j_{!}}}{{\to}}D(X)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle i^{*}}}{{\to}}D(Z)

is a fibre and cofibre sequence in
\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}
.
As a consequence, we get a cofibre sequence of endofunctors

j_{!}j^{*}\to{\mathtt{id}}\to i_{*}i^{*}

(6.5)

of
D(X)
.
Furthermore, the pair of functors
(j^{*},i^{*})
is jointly conservative.

∎

The following notion is taken from
[
Zhu25
, Def. 3.1]
(it is a version of the notion with the same name introduced in
[
DG22
]
in the context of algebraic geometry).

Definition 6.16
.

1.

A functor
D:\mathrm{LCH}\to{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty})
is called a coefficient system
if it is presentable and stable, compatible with
I
, and satisfies canonical descent.

2.

A morphism between coefficient systems
is a natural transformation of functors
which is compatible with
I
(in the sense of
6.6
.
1
) and whose components are morphisms in
{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}})
.

Theorem 6.17
.

The functor

E:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty})

introduced in
6.11
is a coefficient system.

Proof.

By
Theorem

6.12
,
we have a six-functor formalism

E:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pr}_{\mathrm{st}}^{L}\ .

It is presentable and stable and satisfies the axioms of canonical descent by
Theorem

4.12
and
Theorem

4.13
.
∎

In
[
BV
]
, we have shown that for every
coefficient system
D
there is a canonical natural
D(\mathrm{pt})
-linear transformation of coefficient systems

{\mathcal{B}}:\mathrm{Shv}(-,D(\mathrm{pt}))\to D(-)\ .

(6.6)

Remark 6.18
.

Let
D:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{CAlg}}({\mathbf{Cat}}_{\infty})
be a six-functor formalism on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
. For

X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
and
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
we consider the inclusion
j_{U\to X}:U\to X
and the projection
p_{U}:U\to*
.
If
D
is, in addition, a coefficient system, then the association

\mathrm{Open}(X)\ni U\mapsto j_{U\to X,!}p_{U}^{*}1_{D(\mathrm{pt})}\in D(X)

defines a cosheaf
in
\mathrm{CoShv}(X,D(X))
. The colimit-preserving
D(\mathrm{pt})
-linear functor

{\mathcal{B}}_{X}:\mathrm{Shv}(X,D(\mathrm{pt}))\to D(X)

corresponds to this cosheaf under the equivalence

{\mathbf{Fun}}_{D(\mathrm{pt})}^{\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}}(\mathrm{Shv}(X,D(\mathrm{pt})),D(X))\simeq\mathrm{CoShv}(X,D(X))\ .

∎

Note that
\mathrm{E}\simeq E(\mathrm{pt})
.

Corollary 6.19
.

We have a natural transformation

{\mathcal{B}}:\mathrm{Shv}(-,\mathrm{E})\to E:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}})\ .

(6.7)

Definition 6.20
.

For
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
and
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
we define the evaluation

{\mathtt{ev}}_{U}:=p_{U,*}j_{U\to X}^{*}:D(X)\to D(\mathrm{pt})\ .

(6.8)

Definition 6.21
.

1.

We say that
D
is section-determined on
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
if the collection
({\mathtt{ev}}_{U})_{U\in\mathrm{Open}(X)}
of evaluation maps is jointly conservative.

2.

We say that
D
is section-determined if it is section-determined on every
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
.

For a six-functor formalism
D
which is also a coefficient system
D
, we define, for every
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
, a functor

s_{X}:D(X)\to\mathrm{CoShv}(X,D(\mathrm{pt}))\ ,\quad s_{X}(A):U\mapsto p_{U,!}j_{U\to X}^{*}A\ .

(6.9)

As in the proof of
Theorem

4.18
, the cosheaf conditions are implied by the assumption that
D
is a coefficient system.
Note that, in contrast to
{\mathtt{ev}}_{U}(A)=p_{U,*}j_{U\to X}^{*}A
, the definition of

s_{X}(A)(U)
involves
p_{U,!}
.

Proposition 6.22
.

Assume that
D
is a six-functor formalism on
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
which is also a coefficient system
D
.
Then
D
is section-determined if and only if, for every
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
the functor
s_{X}
in (
6.9
) is conservative.,

Proof.

Lemma 6.23
.

Let
D
be a six-functor formalism on
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
which is also a coefficient system.
For every
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
,
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
, and
A
in
D(X)
we have an equivalence

{\mathtt{ev}}_{U}(A)\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{K\subset U}\mathrm{Cofib}(s_{X}(A)(U\setminus K)\to s_{X}(A)(U))\ ,

(6.10)

where
K
runs over the compact subsets of
U
.

Proof.

For every
B
in
D(\mathrm{pt})
we have, by definition of
{\mathtt{ev}}_{U}
,

{\mathtt{map}}_{D(\mathrm{pt})}(B,{\mathtt{ev}}_{U}(A))\simeq{\mathtt{map}}_{D(\mathrm{pt})}(B,p_{U,*}j_{U\to X}^{*}A)\ .

We now use the defining adjunctions of the operations to get

{\mathtt{map}}_{D(\mathrm{pt})}(B,p_{U,*}j_{U\to X}^{*}A)\simeq{\mathtt{map}}_{D(U)}(p_{U}^{*}B,j_{U\to X}^{*}A)\simeq{\mathtt{map}}_{D(X)}(j_{U\to X,!}p_{U}^{*}B,A)\ .

We now use that
U=\bigcup_{V\Subset U}V
and
the fact that for a coefficient system, we have

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{V\Subset U}j_{V\to X,!}j_{V\to X}^{*}\simeq j_{U\to X,!}j_{U\to X}^{*}

in order to
get

{\mathtt{map}}_{D(X)}(j_{U\to X,!}p_{U}^{*}B,A)\simeq{\mathtt{map}}_{D(X)}(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{V\Subset U}j_{V\to X,!}p_{V}^{*}B,A)\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{V\Subset U}{\mathtt{map}}_{D(V)}(j_{V\to X}^{*}p_{X}^{*}B,j_{V\to X}^{*}A)\ .

Using that
V\Subset U
implies
V\subseteq\bar{V}\subseteq U
and that
\bar{V}
is compact,
we can rewrite the limit as

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{V\Subset U}{\mathtt{map}}_{D(V)}(j_{V\to X}^{*}p_{X}^{*}B,j_{V\to X}^{*}A)\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{K\subseteq U}{\mathtt{map}}_{D(K)}(j_{K\to X}^{*}p_{X}^{*}B,j_{K\to X}^{*}A)\ .

Using that
K\to X
is a closed inclusion and
p_{K}:K\to*

is proper, we get

\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{K\subseteq U}{\mathtt{map}}_{D(K)}(j_{K\to X}^{*}p_{X}^{*}B,j_{K\to X}^{*}A)\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{K\subseteq U}{\mathtt{map}}_{D(\mathrm{pt})}(B,p_{K,!}j_{K\to X}^{*}A)\ .

Using the sequence (
4.6
) for
U\setminus K\to U\leftarrow K

we get

p_{K,!}j_{K\to X}^{*}A\simeq\mathrm{Cofib}(s(A)(U\setminus K)\to s(A)(U))\ .

Inserting this and combining all the equivalences we get

{\mathtt{map}}_{D(\mathrm{pt})}(B,{\mathtt{ev}}_{U}(A))\simeq{\mathtt{map}}_{D(\mathrm{pt})}(B,\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{K\subseteq U}\mathrm{Cofib}(s_{X}(A)(U\setminus K)\to s_{X}(A)(U)))\ .

This implies the assertion.
∎

We have a Verdier duality equivalence

{\mathcal{V}}:\mathrm{Shv}(X,D(\mathrm{pt}))\leftrightarrows\mathrm{CoShv}(X,D(\mathrm{pt})):{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}\ .

The right-hand side of the formula (
6.10
)
is precisely the formula
for
{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(s_{X}(A))(U)
.

Assume that
D
is section-determined.
Let
f:A\to B
be a morphism in
D(X)
. If
s_{X}(f)
is an equivalence,
then by
6.23
, we conclude that
{\mathtt{ev}}_{U}(f)
is an equivalence for every
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
.
By the assumption on
D
we conclude that
f
is an equivalence.
This shows that
s_{X}
is conservative.

Assume now that
s_{X}
is conservative. If
{\mathtt{ev}}_{U}(f)
is an
equivalence for every
U
in
\mathrm{Open}(X)
, then
{\mathcal{V}}^{-1}(s_{X}(f))
is an equivalence. Hence, also
s_{X}(f)
is an equivalence.
We conclude that
f
is an equivalence by the assumption on
D
.
This shows that
D
is section-determined.
∎

Theorem 6.24
.

The functor
E
in (
6.3
) is section-determined.

Proof.

By
Theorem

6.17

and
Theorem

6.12
, the
E
-theory functor is a six-functor formalism on
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
and a coefficient system. In
Theorem

5.57
we have shown that
s_{X}:E(X)\to\mathrm{CoShv}(X,\mathrm{E})
is conservative for every
X
in
\mathrm{LCH}
. We now apply
6.22
in order to conclude that
E
is section-determined.
∎

We consider a functor

D:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{CAlg}}(\mathbf{Pr}^{L})
. Then, for every morphism
f
in
\mathrm{LCH}
we have an adjunction
f^{*}\dashv f_{*}
.

Definition 6.25
.

We define the cohomology functor associated with
D
by

\Gamma^{D}:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to D(\mathrm{pt})\ ,\quad X\mapsto p_{X,*}p_{X}^{*}1\ .

Here

1
is the tensor unit in
D(\mathrm{pt})
.

Let
H:\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
be some functor.

Definition 6.26
.

We say that
H
is finitary
1
1

1

In
[
KNP
]
this property is called ”profinite descent”.
if, for every cofiltered system
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathbf{CH}

with
X:=\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in I}X_{i}
, we have
an equivalence

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I^{\mathrm{op}}}H(X_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}H(X)\ .

Proposition 6.27
.

The cohomology functor associated with
E
by is finitary.

Proof.

We have a functor

\Gamma^{(-)C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}}:\mathbf{CH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\ ,\quad X\mapsto p_{X,*}p_{X}^{*}1\ .

Explicitly, this functor is given by
X\mapsto C(X)
. We conclude that the restriction

\Gamma^{E}_{|\mathbf{CH}^{\mathrm{op}}}:\mathbf{CH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{E}

of the cohomology functor to compact Hausdorff spaces is given by

X\mapsto\mathrm{e}(C(X)),\quad\left(f:X\to Y)\mapsto\mathrm{e}(f^{*}):\mathrm{e}(C(Y))\to\mathrm{e}(C(X))\right)\ .

For every cofiltered system
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
in
\mathbf{CH}

with
X:=\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{i\in I}X_{i}
, we have, by Gelfand duality,

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I^{\mathrm{op}}}C(X_{i})\cong C(X)

in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Since the functor
\mathrm{e}:C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{E}
preserves filtered colimits, we conclude that

\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I^{\mathrm{op}}}\mathrm{e}(C(X_{i}))\simeq\mathrm{e}(C(X))\ .

This shows that
\Gamma^{E}
is finitary.
∎

The following theorem is shown in
[
BV
]
.

Theorem 6.28
.

Assume that

D
is a presentable and stable six-functor formalism on the Nagata context
(\mathrm{LCH},I,P)
which is a coefficient system, section-determined, and is such that
D(\mathrm{pt})
is dualizable and its
associated cohomology
\Gamma^{D}
is finitary.
Then the transformation in (
6.6
)
is an equivalence of six-functor formalisms.

Corollary 6.29
.

The transformation (
6.7
) is an equivalence of six-functor formalisms

{\mathcal{B}}:\mathrm{Shv}(-,\mathrm{E})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\simeq}}{{\to}}E(-):\mathrm{LCH}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Pr}^{L}_{\mathrm{st}}\ .

Proof.

The assumptions of (
6.28
) are satisfied:

1.

E
is stable and presentable by construction.

2.

E
is a coefficient system by
Theorem

6.17
.

3.

E
is section-determined by
Theorem

6.24
.

4.

E(\mathrm{pt})\simeq\mathrm{E}
is dualizable by
[
BD24
]
.

5.

\Gamma^{E}
is finitary by
6.27
.

∎

7
Technical lemmas

The following algebraic and categorical properties of
C^{*}
-algebras and their maximal tensor products are well-known standard results. We sketch the arguments for the reader’s convenience.
We state the results precisely in the form in which they are used in
Section

3
.
A good reference for the exactness of the maximal tensor product is
[
BO08
, Prop. 3.7.1]
.

Lemma 7.1
.

Filtered colimits preserve exact sequences.

Proof.

Let

(0\to I_{i}\to A_{i}\to Q_{i}\to 0)_{i\in I}

be a filtered system of exact sequences in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.
Then the assertion is that

0\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}I_{i}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}A_{i}\to\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{i\in I}Q_{i}\to 0

is again exact. This is a well-known fact from
C^{*}
-algebra theory. See e.g.
[
BEL
, Lem. 7.20]

for an argument that even works for
C^{*}
-categories.
∎

If
C^{\prime}\to C
is a homomorphism of
C^{*}
-algebra, we let
\bar{C}^{\prime}
denote the image of
the homomorphism and
\langle\bar{C}^{\prime}\rangle
be the ideal generated by
\bar{C}^{\prime}
.
Recall that
\otimes
denotes the maximal tensor product in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

If
A^{\prime}\to A
and
B^{\prime}\to B
are homomorphisms of
C^{*}
-algebras,
then we have
a canonical isomorphism of ideals

\langle\overline{A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}}\rangle\cong\langle\bar{A}^{\prime}\rangle\otimes\langle\bar{B}^{\prime}\rangle

in
A\otimes B
.

Proof.

It follows from the exactness of
\otimes
that it preserves ideal inclusions in both arguments.
Consequently
\langle\bar{A}^{\prime}\rangle\otimes\langle\bar{B}^{\prime}\rangle
is naturally an ideal in
A\otimes B
.
The ideal
\langle\overline{A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}}\rangle
is the smallest ideal
of
A\otimes B
that contains the images
\overline{a\otimes b}

of
a\otimes b
in
A^{\prime}\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B^{\prime}
for all

a
in
A^{\prime}
and
b
in
B^{\prime}
. Such an element is also the image in
A\otimes B
of

\bar{a}\otimes\bar{b}
in
A\otimes^{\mathrm{alg}}B
.
This implies

\langle\overline{A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}}\rangle\subseteq\langle\bar{A}^{\prime}\rangle\otimes\langle\bar{B}^{\prime}\rangle\ .

Every element of
\langle\bar{A}^{\prime}\rangle\otimes\langle\bar{B}^{\prime}\rangle
be be approximated by sums of elements of the form

x\bar{a}x^{\prime}\otimes y\bar{b}y^{\prime}
for
a
in
A^{\prime}
,
B
in
B^{\prime}
,
x,x^{\prime}
in
A
and
y,y^{\prime}
in
B
.
We consider the elements

(u\otimes y)(x\otimes v)(\overline{a\otimes b})(x^{\prime}\otimes v)(u\otimes y^{\prime})
in

\langle\overline{A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}}\rangle
.
Letting
u
and
v
run over approximate units in
A
and
B
, respectively,
we see that these elements approximate
x\bar{a}x^{\prime}\otimes y\bar{b}y^{\prime}
.
We conclude that

\langle\bar{A}^{\prime}\rangle\otimes\langle\bar{B}^{\prime}\rangle\subseteq\langle\overline{A^{\prime}\otimes B^{\prime}}\rangle\ .

∎

Lemma 7.3
.

If

0\to A\to B\to C\to 0
is an exact sequence in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
,
J
is an ideal in
B

and
I:=A\cap J
, then the canonical map
J/I\to C
is the inclusion of an ideal.

Proof.

We have a map of exact sequences

0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 155.66306pt\raise-31.66666pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

By a diagram chase, we see that the right vertical map is injective.
If
[j]
is in
J/I
and
c
is in
C
, then
c[j]=[cj]\in J/I
. Hence
J/I
is an ideal in
C
.
∎

For the following statements, we let
A,B
be in
C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}
and
I,J
be ideals in
A
, and let
K,L
be ideals in
B
.

Lemma 7.4
.

The canonical map
I\otimes K\to A\otimes B
is an inclusion.

Proof.

The functor
-\otimes B
is exact. It sends the exact sequence

0\to I\to A\to A/I\to 0

to

0\to I\otimes B\to A\otimes B\to A/I\otimes B\to 0\ .

We can apply this also to the functor
I\otimes-
.
Hence

I\otimes K\to I\otimes B\to A\otimes B

is a composition of inclusions.

∎

Lemma 7.5
.

The natural inclusion
(I\cap J)\otimes B\to(I\otimes B)\cap(J\otimes B)
is an equality.

Proof.

We have a cartesian square

\textstyle{I\cap J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{J\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{I+J}

of ideals in
A
, meaning in particular that
I+J
is again a closed ideal.
The canonical map of quotients
I/(I\cap J)\to(I+J)/J
is an isomorphism.

We get a square of ideals

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 28.44263pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-28.44263pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(I\cap J)\otimes B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.69608pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.66669pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 65.69608pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{I\otimes B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 81.44081pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-16.40794pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{J\otimes B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.44263pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 52.44263pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(I+J)\otimes B}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

All maps remain injective, and the canonical map of quotients

I\otimes B/((I\cap J)\otimes B)\to((I+J)\otimes B)/(J\otimes B)

is still an isomorphism.
This implies that the square is still cartesian which implies the assertion.
∎

Lemma 7.6
.

The natural inclusion

(I\cap J)\otimes(K\cap L)\to(I\otimes K)\cap(J\otimes L)

of ideals in
A\otimes B
is an equality.

Proof.

We consider the diagram

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(I\cap J)\otimes(K\cap L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.85059pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.31058pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 85.31058pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\otimes(K\cap L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern-24.89616pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 101.16989pt\raise-8.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-40.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 112.62302pt\raise-64.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 217.25822pt\raise-64.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\ .

The two horizontal sequences are exact. By a diagram chase, we get the dotted arrow.
We now show that

(I\otimes K)\cap(J\otimes L)\subseteq A\otimes(K\cap L)\cap(I\cap J)\otimes B\ .

We have inclusions

(I\otimes K)\cap(J\otimes L)\subseteq(I\otimes B)\cap(J\otimes B)=(I\cap J)\otimes B\ ,

where we use
7.5
for the second equality.
Analogously

(I\otimes K)\cap(J\otimes L)\subseteq A\otimes(K\cap L)\ .

This implies

(I\otimes K)\cap(J\otimes L)\subseteq(I\cap J)\otimes(K\cap L)

and hence the assertion.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
(I_{i})_{i}
be a family of ideals in a
C^{*}
-algebra
A
, and
B
be a further
C^{*}
-algebra. Then
we have

\overline{\sum_{i\in I}I_{i}}\otimes B=\overline{\sum_{i\in I}I_{i}\otimes B}

inside

A\otimes B
.

Proof.

We use that
-\otimes B
preserves ideal inclusions, finite sums of ideals, and filtered colimits.
∎

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
A
be a
C^{*}
-algebra and let
I,J,K
be ideals in
A
.
Then
(I\cap J)/(K\cap I\cap J)=I/(K\cap I)\cap J/(K\cap J)
in
A/K
.

Proof.

We have a canonical inclusion map

(I\cap J)/(K\cap I\cap J)\to I/(K\cap I)\cap J/(K\cap J)
. We must show that it is surjective.
Let
q
be in
I/(K\cap I)\cap J/(K\cap J)
. Then there exists

a
in
I
and
b
in
J
such that
[a]=q=[b]
.
Hence
a+d=b
for some
d
in
K\cap J
. It follows
a=b-d
which implies
d\in K\cap I
.
Hence
d\in K\cap I\cap J
and
[b]=[a+d]=q
. Furthermore,
b\in I\cap J
.
The class of
b
is the desired preimage of
q
.

∎

Lemma 7.9
.

Let
I,J,K
be ideals in
A
.
Then

(I\cap J)+K=(I+K)\cap(J+K)\ .

Proof.

The lemma says that
the ideals in a
C^{*}
-algebra constitute a distributive lattice.
This follows from the identification of the poset
I(A)
with
\mathrm{Open}(\mathrm{Prim}(A))
(see
[
Dix83
, Sec. 2.9]
).
First observe that

(I\cap J)+K\subseteq(I+K)\cap(J+K)\ .

Assume now that

i+k=j+k^{\prime}\in(I+K)\cap(J+K)\ .

Let
(u)
be an approximate unit of
I+K
.
We have
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{u}u(i+k)=i+k
. On the other hand,

u(i+k)=u(j+k^{\prime})\in(I+K)(J+K)=IJ+K\subseteq(I\cap J)+K\ .

Hence
i+k\in(I\cap J)+K
.

∎

Lemma 7.10
.

If
0\to I\to A\to Q\to 0
and
0\to J\to B\to P
are exact sequences, then

0\to I\otimes B+A\otimes J\to A\otimes B\to Q\otimes P\to 0

is exact.

Proof.

This follows from the exactness of the maximal tensor product.
∎
